                                 Preface

ATP 3-09.23 sets forth the doctrine pertaining to the organization, equipment, command and control,
operations, and techniques for the field artillery battalion in support of a maneuver or supported commander.
It establishes the responsibilities and general duties of key personnel by focusing on techniques of how the
unit fights. This document incorporates procedures outlined for all field artillery battalions.

The principal audience for ATP 3-09.23 is all members of the artillery community. This includes field
artillery Soldiers and leaders. Commanders and staffs of Army headquarters serving as joint task force or
multinational headquarters will also refer to applicable joint or multinational doctrine concerning the range
of military operations and joint or multinational forces. Trainers and educators throughout the Army will also
use this publication.

This publication describes the field artillery battalion to include:
     • Doctrinal guidance for commanders, staffs, and subordinate personnel that are responsible for
          planning, preparing, executing, and assessing field artillery operations.
     • Operations for the field artillery battalions assigned, attached, or under the operational control of
          the corps, division, brigade combat team, division artillery, field artillery brigade, or multidomain
          task force. The techniques described herein are flexible guidelines.

Commanders, staffs, and subordinates must ensure that their decisions and actions comply with applicable
U.S., international, and in some cases host-nation laws and regulations. Commanders at all levels will ensure
that their Soldiers operate in accordance with the law of armed conflict and applicable rules of engagement.
(See FM 6-27)

ATP 3-09.23 implements North Atlantic Treaty Organization Standard Agreement (STANAG) 2934.

ATP 3-09.23 uses joint terms where applicable. Selected joint and Army terms and definitions appear in both
the glossary and the text. Terms for which ATP 3-09.23 is the proponent publication (the authority) are
marked with an asterisk (*) in the glossary. Terms for which ATP 3-09.23 is the proponent publication are
bold faced in the text. For other definitions shown in the text, the term is italicized and the number of the
proponent publication follows the definition.

ATP 3-09.23 applies to the Active Army, Army National Guard/Army National Guard of the United States
and United States Army Reserve unless otherwise stated.

The proponent of ATP 3-09.23 is the Fires Center of Excellence. The preparing agency is the United States
Army Field Artillery School. Send comments and recommendations on DA Form 2028 (Recommended
Changes to Publications and Blank Forms) to Directorate of Training and Doctrine, ATTN: ATSF-DD (ATP
3-09.23), 700 McNair Avenue, Suite 117, OK 73505-4456; or by e-mail to usarmy.sill.fcoe.mbx.dotd-
doctrine-inbox@army.mil; or submit an electronic DA Form 2028.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  vii

                              Introduction

The techniques herein build on the collective knowledge and experience gained through operations,
numerous exercises, and the deliberate process of informed reasoning. These techniques are rooted in the
fire’s principles identified in ADP 3-19 and FM 3-09 and accommodate force design changes, new
technologies, and diverse threats.

ATP 3-09.23 has eight chapters and three appendices.

Chapter 1 provides the battalion organizational framework for the field artillery battalion.
Chapter 2 describes field artillery battalion command and control.
Chapter 3 provides an overview of the field artillery battalion planning process.
Chapter 4 describes internal and external information collection support to the detect function.
Chapter 5 focuses on the execution of attack with fires.
Chapter 6 discusses how a field artillery battalion operates.
Chapter 7 describes the sustainment warfighting function as it relates to the field artillery battalion.
Chapter 8 identifies the unique considerations and characteristics of the different types of field artillery
battalions.

Appendix A discusses development, format, and content of a formal field artillery battalion operation order
and field artillery support plan.

Appendix B provides an overview of liaison duties.
Appendix C discusses the requirement for distributed units.
Based on current doctrinal changes, no terms have been added, modified, or rescinded. The glossary contains
acronyms and defined terms.

                                                 Chapter 1

      Field Artillery Battalion Organizational Framework

    This chapter provides the organizational framework for the field artillery (FA) battalion.
    Section I describes the FA battalion organization. Section II describes the headquarters and
    headquarters battery (HHB). Section III lists key FA battalion personnel and responsibilities.

SECTION I – FIELD ARTILLERY BATTALION ORGANIZATION

     1-1. FA battalions are organized with a HHB and two to three firing batteries. HHB and the forward support
     company (FSC) provide command and control (C2), administrative, and logistical support for organic and
     attached elements. Each firing battery has fire direction centers (FDC) and cannon or rocket sections. A FSC
     from the brigade support battalion (BSB) supports the FA battalion.

THE FIELD ARTILLERY BATTALION

     1-2. The FA battalion suppresses, neutralizes, or destroys the enemy by cannon, rocket, and missile fires to
     enable the supported commander to dominate in large-scale combat operations. The FA battalion also
     provides counterfire against enemy mortar, cannon, and rocket elements.

     1-3. The FA battalion has a specific support relationship to the supported commander. Unique characteristics
     of each different type of FA battalions are identified in chapter 8.

     1-4. The FA cannon battalion provides indirect fire support (FS) to maneuver or supported forces on the
     battlefield. The FA rocket and missile battalions enable the field artillery brigade (FAB) to conduct corps
     level strike and division level shaping operations. The multidomain task force (MDTF) has a long-range fires
     battalion that provides operational, theater strategic, and strategic fires for the joint force. A command
     relationship is established with a senior headquarters (HQ), and a support relationship is assigned each
     battalion.

     1-5. The security force assistance brigade (SFAB) FA battalion develops the fires capability and capacity of
     foreign security forces to conduct targeting, deliver fires, and integrate fires at multiple echelons within the
     operational environment. Advisor teams advise and train the foreign security forces in the application of
     indirect fires and must be prepared to support them with indirect fires through coordination of joint fire assets,
     if necessary. The SFAB FA battalion does not have organic cannon or rocket artillery systems and thus no
     firing capabilities. For more information on SFAB FA battalion operations see ATP 3-96.1.

ORGANIZATION

     1-6. FA battalions are task organized based on their assigned duties and aligned with a specific brigade
     combat team (BCT), division artillery (DIVARTY), FAB, or a multi-domain task force. The different types
     of battalions are:

          • Composite (105 millimeter [mm]) and 155mm towed).
          • Composite missile battalion (long-range fires battalion).
          • 155mm towed.
          • 155mm self-propelled (SP).
          • High mobility artillery rocket system (HIMARS).
          • Multiple launch rocket system (MLRS).
          • Army National Guard (ARNG) Composite battalion.
          • ARNG 155mm towed cannon.
          • ARNG 155mm SP cannon.
          • ARNG HIMARS (2x8) and (3x9).
          • ARNG MLRS (2x8) and (3x9).
          • Security Force Assistance Brigade Field Artillery Battalion (no firing capabilities).

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  1


---

Chapter 1

     1-7. FA battalions are attached, assigned, or organic to BCTs, DIVARTYs, FABs, and multidomain task
     forces. All FA battalions have an HHB to provide C2 and administrative support. A FSC provides logistical
     support. The battalion is organized with two to three firing batteries. The battalion provides responsive and
     accurate fires to the supported commander. See figure 1-1 for an example of a generic FA battalion.

                               Figure 1-1. Generic field artillery battalion example

    SECTION II – HEADQUARTERS AND HEADQUARTERS BATTERY
    ORGANIZATION

     1-8. The HHB is organic to the battalion and is organized and equipped to coordinate administrative,
     logistical, maintenance, and communications support for the battalion HQ and firing batteries. The HHB
     provides command, control, administrative, and service support for organic and attached elements. It also
     procures, distributes, administers, and coordinates supply transactions for all classes of supply, and
     coordinates unit maintenance support from the FSC and higher-level sustainment organizations.

     1-9. The HHB operates as a tactical and administrative HQ. The HQ element will be positioned for the most
     effective control of the battalion assets commensurate with the terrain, combat mission and responsiveness
     to the higher HQ, and supported units. The functional elements of the HHB are discussed below.

HEADQUARTERS AND HEADQUARTERS BATTERY

     1-10. The HHB is configured to provide the battalion with C2. The target acquisition platoon and the fire
     support element (FSE) are found only in FA cannon battalions. See figure 1-2 on page 3 for an example of
     an HHB.

2  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                  Field Artillery Battalion Organizational Framework

                               Figure 1-2. Headquarters and headquarters battery

BATTERY HEADQUARTERS

     1-11. The battery HQ provides command, control, and supervision for the battery. The HQ consists of the
     battery commander, first sergeant (1SG), and one driver.

BATTERY SUPPLY SECTION

     1-12. The supply section consists of a supply sergeant (SGT) and a supply specialist. The supply section
     coordinates with the battalion S-4 to obtain all classes of supplies from the FSC. The FSC provides the supply
     section with supplies forward with the next logistics packages, or immediately if needed.

BATTALION COMMAND SECTION

     1-13. The battalion command section provides command, supervision, and control for all tactical and
     administrative operations of the battalion. The section consists of the battalion commander, the executive
     officer (XO), and the command sergeant major (CSM). The HQ controls and coordinates battalion activities.

BATTALION FIRE DIRECTION CENTER

     1-14. The battalion FDC has tactical control over and provides tactical fire direction to the firing batteries.
     The fire direction officer (FDO) supervises the FDC. It is staffed with one senior fire control

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  3


---

Chapter 1

     noncommissioned Officer (NCO), one fire control NCO, one fire control sergeant (SGT), and three to five
     tactical data system operators dependent on the type of FA battalion.

     1-15. The battalion FDC provides tactical fire planning and fire control through automated systems with
     manual backup and communications equipment. Specific responsibilities are as follows:

          • Monitor and operate in the battalion fire direction and FS coordination nets.
          • Schedule fire units for planned fires in coordination with the battalion operations staff officer,

                brigade fire support officer (FSO) (if applicable), reinforcing (R) or reinforced FA (as appropriate),
                and force field artillery HQ.
          • Review the maneuver commander's attack guidance and force field artillery HQ directives and apply
                them to all fire mission requests.
          • Ensure all battalion elements have the proper guidance and attack criteria entered into digital
                systems for both current and planned operations. Where applicable, this includes all FSE and
                requires close coordination with the brigade FSE.
          • Execute planned fires as requested by force field artillery HQ, fires elements, observers, and
                reinforced units.
          • Coordinate fire mission processing procedures (digital and voice) with FSEs, force field artillery
                HQ, reinforced or R units, targeting and intelligence assets, and air defense support element
                (ADSE)/air ground integration element (AGIE) as appropriate (formerly known as the
                ADAM/BAE). This includes digital fire mission routing and primary computer system intervention
                rules. For airspace clearance, this includes digital or voice coordination requests and munition flight
                paths.
          • Respond to immediate fire requests in the priority established by the supported maneuver
                commander's attack criteria.
          • Ensure the battalion meets the five requirements for accurate predicted fires:
                 Accurate target location and size.
                 Accurate firing unit locations.
                 Accurate weapons and munitions information.
                 Accurate meteorological information.
                 Accurate computational procedures.
          • Determine registration requirements in coordination with the battalion or brigade operations staff
                officer (S-3).
          • Provide technical fire direction assistance to battery and platoon FDCs as required.
          • Coordinate for technical fire direction when battery and platoon FDCs have a catastrophic loss of
                technical fire direction capability.
          • Ensure that all fire missions comply with current fire support coordination measures (FSCM) and
                are deconflicted with current airspace coordinating measures (ACM).
          • Assist the S-3 in monitoring ammunition expenditures. In cannon units, this includes ammunition
                lot management. Recommend changes to attack criteria or other tactical fire direction guidance as
                necessary.
          • Conduct mutual support operations as required.
          • Establish and practice operations in a degraded mode.
          • Plan for and employ precision munitions.

BRIGADE FIRE SUPPORT ELEMENT

     1-16. The brigade FSE (cannon battalions only) works closely with battalion FSEs and the fire support team
     (FIST) of each company. These organizations ensure that responsive and effective FS is provided to their
     respective maneuver or supported commanders through their main command post (CP) FSE.

     1-17. The brigade FSE is organized with the FA battalion commander as the brigade fire support coordinator
     (FSCOORD), a brigade FSO and assistants, targeting officer, an ADSE/AGIE, an electromagnetic warfare
     element, and digital systems operators. The FSE may also have an Air Force tactical air control party. These
     personnel are all members of the targeting team.

4  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                               Field Artillery Battalion Organizational Framework

Current Operations Cell

     1-18. The current operations cell is the focal point for controlling the execution of operations. This involves
     assessing the current situation while regulating forces and warfighting functions in accordance with the
     mission, commander’s intent, and concept of operations. The chief of operations or assistant S-3 leads this
     cell. Elements or watch officers from each staff section and liaison officers (LNO) from subordinate and
     adjacent units form this cell. All staff sections are represented in the current operations integrating cell either
     permanently or on call (ATP 6-0.5).

Future Operations Cell

     1-19. The future operations cell is responsible for planning operations in the mid-range planning horizon.
     This cell focuses on adjustments to current operations, including the positioning or maneuvering of forces in
     depth that facilitate continuation of current operations. The cell consists of a core group of planners led by
     the future operations officer. All staff sections assist as required. (ATP 6-0.5)

Battalion Fire Support Element

     1-20. Battalion FSEs in the maneuver battalions provide a FS coordination capability for battalion operations
     and are organized with an FSO and FS NCO, and digital systems operators. The FSE may also have an Air
     Force tactical air control party.

Fire Support Platoon

     1-21. Company FIST HQ personnel and platoon forward observers enable effective artillery and mortar
     support for the maneuver force. The observers are the ears and eyes of the FA and a major source of
     information for FA and maneuver commanders as the observers execute assigned tasks and respond to enemy
     actions.

     1-22. FIST is a FA team providing support to each maneuver battalion and selected units to plan and
     coordinate all available supporting fires, including mortars, FA, naval surface FS and close air support (CAS)
     integration. FISTs are assigned to the cannon FA battalion and provide the maneuver companies with FS
     coordination, precision targeting, assist in targeting for CAS and effects assessment capabilities.

     1-23. Effective FA fires require certified observers to call for and adjust fires on located targets. Forward
     observers, forward air controllers, naval gunfire spotter teams, joint fires observers (JFO) and joint terminal
     attack controllers (JTAC) train together and work effectively as a team to request, plan, coordinate, and place
     accurate fires on targets that create the effects desired by the supported commander.

     1-24. A forward observer is an individual operating with front line troops trained to adjust ground or naval
     gunfire and pass back battlefield information (JP 3-09). Platoon forward observers are assigned to the FIST
     supporting each infantry company in the BCT.

     1-25. A joint fires observer is a certified and qualified Service member who requests, controls, and adjusts
     surface-to-surface fires; provides targeting information in support of close air support; and performs terminal
     guidance operations (JP 3-09.3). JFOs cannot perform terminal attack control of CAS missions and do not
     replace a qualified JTACs/forward air controller (airborne). Trained Service members will be jointly known
     as a JFO.

S-1 SECTION

     1-26. The personnel staff section provides administrative and legal support and helps the battalion
     commander provide for the welfare of the battalion personnel. The battalion or brigade personnel staff officer
     (S-1) supervises the section. The section includes a senior human resources SGT, two human resources SGTs,
     a paralegal specialist, and four human resources specialists.

S-2 SECTION

     1-27. The intelligence staff section processes battlefield information and coordinates information collection
     operations within the battalion and with the supported maneuver units and military intelligence assets. The

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  5


---

Chapter 1

     battalion or brigade intelligence staff officer (S-2) supervises the section, which includes a targeting officer
     (rocket battalion only), intelligence SGT, intelligence analyst, and tactical data system operator.

     1-28. The intelligence section provides intelligence and security information and conducts FA focused
     intelligence preparation of the operational environment (IPOE). The section tracks and creates the priority
     intelligence requirements (PIR) related to FS, the commander's intent, and the mission statement. They
     develop the PIRs related to the battalion protection warfighting functions. The S-2 manages all attached target
     acquisition systems and Army, joint, and national sensor system down links under the battalion's operational
     control (OPCON).

     1-29. The S-2 section processes and correlates targeting data to include predicting and monitoring assigned
     high-payoff target (HPT) locations and passes this information to the controlling FA HQ and the battalion
     FDC. They coordinate with the S-3 and counterfire cell to determine potential enemy position area for
     artillery (PAA) based on terrain during the IPOE process. The S-2 coordinates the battalion ground and air
     defense plans with the batteries and coordinates with battalion targeting officer to plan and execute zone
     coverage and cueing schedules for all attached weapons locating radars (WLR).

S-3 SECTION

     1-30. The S-3 battalion operations staff office supervises the CP, and the operations S-3 section. The section
     coordinates all tactical, technical, and logistical operations and planning for the battalion. The section
     employs the batteries to meet the needs of the supported units. It develops warning orders, the operation order
     (OPORD), and fragmentary orders; maintains the tactical situation maps and overlays; and coordinates
     tactical movements and positioning. The section is staffed with the operations officer, operations SGT, master
     gunner/assistant operations SGT, chemical, biological, radiological and nuclear (CBRN) officer, two CBRN
     NCOs, two tactical data system operators, and a CBRN decontamination specialist.

S-4 SECTION

     1-31. The battalion or brigade logistics staff officer (S-4) supervises the battalion S-4 section and serves as a
     shift leader. The section establishes the administrative and logistics operations center and coordinates the
     overall logistical activities of the battalion. It works closely with the operations section in monitoring the
     resupply of ammunition and fuel. The section includes a senior maintenance supervisor, a senior supply SGT,
     and four supply Specialists.

Ammunition Management Section

     1-32. The battalion ammunition officer monitors the ammunition status of the battalion and manages all
     aspects of ammunition resupply. The section is composed of the Ammunition Officer, Ammunition SGT,
     and a vehicle driver.

S-6 SECTION

     1-33. The battalion or brigade signal staff officer (S-6) is the principal staff officer for all matters concerning
     signal operations, automation management, network operations, communications security (COMSEC),
     electromagnetic spectrum management, and information security. The S-6 plans signal operations, prepares
     the signal annex to the OPORDs, and recommends employment of communications assets in support of
     operations. The section attaches support teams forward with the firing batteries. The signal section consists
     of the signal support systems chief, two forward signal support NCOs, one signal information service
     specialist, and one signal support system specialist.

Network Management Team

     1-34. Network Management Team is responsible for management of the battalion local area network. The
     section is composed of two local area network managers. The network operations team install, operate, and
     maintain the CP node. The CP node provides voice and data connectivity to the Department of Defense
     information network-Army for information systems. The team is comprised of a senior transmissions systems
     operator and maintainer and one transmissions systems operator maintainer.

6  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                               Field Artillery Battalion Organizational Framework

Network Extension

     1-35. The retransmission team establishes and maintains the retransmission station, as required. Dual
     retransmission capability is essential to maintaining both voice and data communications over extended
     distances. The section is composed of the team chief and two radio retransmission operators, 1 radio
     retransmission supervisor, 1 team chief, 4 radio retransmission operators.

     1-36. The Communication System: the Soldier Network Extension (SNE) is a Warfighter Information
     Network-Tactical Equipment (WIN-T) communications package for selected vehicles at the battalion and
     company level. The SNE provides on-the-move satellite communication connectivity to the WIN-T network
     and provides healing for fragmented dismounted terrestrial networks and simultaneously supports reach-back
     to higher echelons. The SNE provides a combat network radio gateway function, connecting tactical radio
     voice networks to the WIN-T network. The SNE employs active congestion control and utilizes a proxy to
     enhance the performance of traffic over high-delay satellite links.

Network Operations

     1-37. Department of Defense information network operations are operations to secure, configure, operate,
     extend, maintain, and sustain Department of Defense cyberspace to create and preserve the confidentiality,
     availability, and integrity of the Department of Defense information network (JP 3-12).

     1-38. The network operations and security center provide guidance to subordinate signal elements through
     technical channels. The network operations and security center monitor the health of the network and directs
     fault, configuration, accounting, performance, and security management. The network operations team
     consists of one senior network communications system specialist, and three additional network
     communications systems specialists.

Liaison Section

     1-39. The liaison section consists of two liaison teams each with three personnel. They are necessary to
     accommodate frequent changes in support relationships, to adequately support unified action partners.

Unit Ministry Team

     1-40. A unit ministry team consists of at least one Chaplain and one Religious Affairs Specialist, based on
     organizational requirements. Religious support facilitates the free exercise of religion, provides religious
     activities that support resiliency efforts to sustain soldiers, Family members and authorized civilians. The
     Unit ministry team advises commands on matters of religion, ethics, morals, and morale and their impact on
     military operations. The Chaplain Corps executes its required religious support mission through three core
     competencies: nurturing the living, caring for the wounded, and honoring the dead.

Target Acquisition Platoon

     1-41. The target acquisition platoon (FA cannon battalion only) provides the FA battalion and the supported
     higher HQ with the capability to acquire threat mortar, artillery, and rocket systems and provide target
     intelligence and information. This provides the FA battalion an organic counterfire mission processing
     capability. The target acquisition platoons are organized with a platoon HQ, a counterfire operations section,
     a radar section, and a lightweight counter mortar radar section. The platoon HQ consists of one platoon leader
     and one platoon SGT. For more detailed descriptions of the target acquisition platoon refer to ATP 3-09.12.

Radar Section

     1-42. The radar section consists of two WLRs. Each WLR is a highly mobile radar set that automatically
     detects, classifies, tracks, and locates the point of origin of projectiles fired from mortar, artillery, and rocket
     systems with sufficient accuracy for first round fire for effect. Each section has a section chief, radar team
     chief, and three WLR specialists.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  7


---

Chapter 1

Counterfire Operations Section

     1-43. This section works together with the battalion intelligence officer and staff to produce targetable data.
     These personnel have the responsibility to perform counterfire operations and to satisfy information
     requirements. The section is made up of a targeting officer, a counterfire NCO, two targeting NCOs and two
     targeting processing specialists.

     1-44. The section is responsible for processing acquisitions, sensor management and maintaining the current
     maintenance status of the WLRs during operations. The section assists with integrating and synchronizing
     the WLR assets in accordance with the commander's intent and concept of operations.

Lightweight Counter Mortar Radar Section

     1-45. The lightweight counter mortar radar is a radar system designed for detecting, verifying, tracking, and
     classifying enemy mortar, cannon, and rocket locations. The section is made up of a section chief, a radar
     team chief, six WLRs specialists.

Survey Team

     1-46. Survey is one of the critical tenants of the five requirements for accurate predicted fires. The use of
     survey provides FA assets with a common grid that will permit the massing of fires. Survey is used as a
     secondary method to establish survey control points (SCPs) (near stake and far stake data) for the WLR. The
     survey team equipment consists of an electronic surveying instrument providing accurate and timely three-
     dimensional position data.

     1-47. The survey team is an additional duty assignment as directed by the commander that provides fifth
     order precision accuracy. Fifth-order survey accuracy standard are 7.0 meters horizontal circular error
     probable, 3.0 meters vertical probable error and no more than 0.4 mil azimuth probable error. The survey
     team provides survey to all FA assets in the battalion and supported units to include establishing SCPs.
     Survey is discussed in greater detail in Chapter 5.

Medical Platoon

     1-48. The medical platoon, also referred to as battalion aid station (BAS), coordinates the Army Health
     System support operations for the battalion commander. The BAS is a forward most medically staffed
     treatment location organic to a maneuver battalion. It is uniquely organized, trained, and equipped to conduct
     medical treatment and evacuation tasks. For more information on medical treatment and evacuation tasks see
     ATP 4-02.4.

Ambulance Team

     1-49. Ambulance teams provide evacuation within the battalion. Each ambulance is equipped with a medical
     equipment set ground ambulance. The principal function of the ambulance team is to collect and treat the
     sick and wounded on the battlefield and to safely evacuate them to the nearest role of care or ambulance
     exchange point.

     1-50. The ambulance team consists of an emergency care SGT, ambulance driver, and an ambulance drivers'
     aide for each battery. Each ambulance team is dedicated to support a specific battery unless otherwise directed
     by the platoon leader. In mass casualty situations, nonmedical vehicles may be used to assist in casualty
     evacuation (CASEVAC) as directed by the commander. Plans for the use of nonmedical vehicles to perform
     CASEVAC should be included in the battalion standard operating procedures (SOP) and during rehearsals.

Combat Medic Section

     1-51. The combat medic section provides a combat medic to each firing platoon to perform immediate life
     saving measures to casualties. Depending on mission variables, during lulls in combat operations combat
     medics should return to the medical platoon for consultation and proficiency training. The mission variables
     are mission, enemy, terrain and weather, troops and support available, time available, civil considerations
     and informational considerations, remembered with the mnemonic METT-TC (I).

8  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                               Field Artillery Battalion Organizational Framework

     1-52. Combat medics habitually work with the same battery. It is often necessary to augment these medics
     with Soldiers who have received enhanced first aid training (for example, combat lifesavers). The goal is to
     have one combat lifesaver (CLS) trained per section throughout the battalion. A combat lifesaver is a
     nonmedical Soldier trained to provide enhanced first aid as a secondary mission. The CLS provides
     emergency care as a secondary mission as their primary (combat) mission allows. The CLS does not replace
     the combat medic; however, they should assist the combat medic in providing far forward care and in
     preparing casualties for evacuation.

     1-53. Aid and litter teams locate, treat, and transport injured individuals and casualties (CASEVAC and
     medical evacuation [MEDEVAC]), freeing medical platoon personnel from nontreatment duties. Patients in
     the triage category: minimal, have relatively minor injuries and are usually ambulatory; they may be used in
     aid and litter teams if medical personnel determine they can be returned to duty immediately.

Treatment Team

     1-54. The medical treatment team is the basic medical treatment element of the BAS. It provides routine sick
     call services, triage, emergency medical care, and advanced medical treatment. This team is staffed with a
     physician assistant, one health care SGT and three health care specialists. The treatment team physician
     assistant, and enlisted health care personnel are all trained in medical treatment procedures, commensurate
     with their positions and skill levels. The medical treatment team establishes the BAS.

FORWARD SUPPORT COMPANY

     1-55. There is one FSC attached to each FA battalion in a FAB, while FSCs are organic to each FA battalion
     in a DIVARTY. FSCs are attached to the FA battalions because they operate in dispersed fashion and
     regularly displace their firing batteries during combat. The FSCs may be attached to the BSB if the situation
     dictates. The FSC commander is responsible for executing the sustainment plan for the fires battalion in
     accordance with the supported fires battalion commander's guidance. The FSC is organic to the BSB and
     augments the FA battalion.

     1-56. The FSCs provide the brigade, battalion, and BSB commanders flexibility for providing logistics
     support. FSCs provide the BSB commander the ability to task, organize, and prioritize the logistics effort to
     support large-scale combat operations.

     1-57. The FSCs have a HQ section, distribution platoon, and a maintenance platoon. The distribution platoon
     consists of battalion and battery support squads. The maintenance platoon consists of a platoon HQ,
     maintenance control section, maintenance section, service and recovery section, and the field maintenance
     teams. See figure 1-3 for an example of an FSC.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  9


---

Chapter 1

                                         Figure 1-3. Forward support company

     1-58. The HQ food service section provides class I support, food service, and food preparation for the
     company and its supported battalion. The food service section prepares, serves, and distributes the full range
     of operational rations.

     1-59. The distribution platoon consists of a platoon HQ and four squads that can be task organized to
     distribute and provide the assets for movement of bulk water, bulk fuel, and the necessary platforms for
     hauling or transporting all classes of supply, to include repair parts as needed. The distribution platoon leader
     and platoon SGT execute all or a portion of the materiel management functions as required ensuring supply
     stocks are available and ready for distribution. These personnel also execute both distribution integration and
     transportation operations functions, as required, to ensure supplies are assigned a transportation mode, route,
     and movement time.

     1-60. The maintenance platoon of the FSC performs field-level maintenance, maintenance management
     functions, dispatching, and scheduled maintenance operations for the FA battalion and FSC vehicles and
     equipment. The platoon consists of the platoon HQ section, maintenance control section, maintenance
     section, service and recovery section, and the field maintenance teams. The FSC maintenance platoon
     coordinates all maintenance requirements with the FSC commander.

SECTION III – FIELD ARTILLERY BATTALION KEY PERSONNEL AND
RESPONSIBILITIES

     1-61. The commander controls the activities of the battalion with the assistance of the battalion staff. The
     battalion XO synchronizes the efforts of the battalion staff. The battalion staff consists of coordinating,
     special, and personal staff officers.

     1-62. The coordinating staff officers include the S-1, S-2, S-3, S-4, and the S-6. The chaplain and CSM are
     members of the commander's personal staff. Special staff officers include the FDO, targeting officer, CBRN
     officer, and physician assistant. The FSC has a distribution platoon leader who has as an additional duty and
     responsibility to function as the FA battalion ammunition officer.

10         ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                               Field Artillery Battalion Organizational Framework

COMMAND GROUP

     1-63. ADP 6-0 identifies the command groups organization and duties. Discussion of the command group
     personnel and duties follow.

Battalion Commander

     1-64. The battalion commander directs employment of the FA battalion in accordance with assigned missions
     and guidance form their higher FA HQ, typically a DIVARTY or corps FAB serving as the echelon force
     field artillery HQ. Aided by the battalion staff the battalion commander directs the tactical, logistical,
     administrative, and training activities of the FA battalion. When the FA battalion is direct support (DS) to a
     maneuver brigade or supported commander, the FA battalion commander serves as that maneuver or
     supported commander’s FSCOORD and plans fires in support of operations.

     1-65. In the role of FSCOORD, the battalion commander is responsible for advising the brigade commander
     and the supported commanders on the employment and training of all FS assets, as required. FM 3-09 details
     the duties of the FSCOORD. The battalion commander directs employment of the FA battalion in accordance
     with assigned missions and guidance from the supported higher HQ. The battalion commander works closely
     with the commanders of supported and supporting units to ensure that the FA battalion can accomplish its
     mission. The battalion commander has specific responsibilities, which include:

          • Oversee the training of the battalion, with particular emphasis on those elements directly concerned
                with delivery of fires and FS.

          • Continually assess the needs of the battalion in terms of its ability to sustain its internal operations
                and to support assigned missions.

          • Advise the brigade commander on the effects of fires on the environment and rules of engagement
                (ROE).

          • Establish clear and consistent standards and guidance for current and future operations.
          • Establish policies to promote discipline and ensure a positive and ethical command climate within

                the battalion.
          • Provide for the administrative and logistical support of the battalion.
          • Coordinate fires digital training with the supported higher HQ FSE and battalion or squadron FSEs,

                as required.
          • Control the operations and fires of FA units.
          • Provide guidance and ensure targeting and lethal effects are applied in an ethical manner.
          • Direct preparation of the FA battalion OPORD/field artillery support plan (FASP) see appendix A.
          • Perform FSCOORD duties, as necessary:

                 Advise the supported/maneuver commander on all FS matters.
                 Direct and supervise the training, planning, and coordination of all FS assets supporting the

                     maneuver unit, to include FSEs and FISTs.
                 Coordinate and control the operations and fires of R and general support-reinforcing (GSR) FA

                     units.
                 Direct and supervise preparation of the FS plan in support of the maneuver plan.

Command Sergeant Major

     1-66. The battalion CSM is the senior enlisted advisor to the commander. The CSM is both a specialist and
     a generalist. The CSM must have technical competence as a FA Soldier while being broadly knowledgeable
     in all functional areas such as operations, administration, and sustainment. The CSM best serves as an
     extension of the commander's eyes and primary troubleshooter. The CSM works closely with each of the
     battalion staff sections and the firing battery leaders, frequently changing the area of focus based on the needs
     of the unit and the direction of the commander. The CSM usually operates independently of, but
     complementary to the commander, frequently at a critical location where the commander needs additional
     supervision, oversight, or observation. Because of this, the CSM requires a vehicle, radio, and driver. The
     CSM has specific responsibilities, which include:

          • Assist the commander in maintaining effective communication with senior and subordinate leaders
                and staffs.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  11


---

Chapter 1

          • Validate that the commander's directions and intent are being properly communicated through the
                leadership chain to the front-line Soldiers.

          • Advise the commander and staff on matters pertaining to enlisted Soldiers.
          • Assist the S-3 in planning, coordinating, and supervising collective and individual training to include

                certification requirements.
          • Mentor unit 1SGs and NCOs.
          • Direct NCO professional development.
          • Develop future leaders from within the enlisted ranks.

Executive Officer

     1-67. As second in command and chief of staff, the XO must keep abreast of current and anticipated
     operations. The XO must be prepared to assume the duties of the commander if the need arises. The XO will
     be positioned forward, traveling among the battalion CP, the batteries, and the trains area(s) to perform their
     assigned duties. The XO has specific responsibilities, which include:

          • Direct staff actions and operations, to include the battalion's military decision-making process
                (MDMP), especially production of the FA battalion OPORD/FASP and battalion staff involvement
                in the brigade FS plan.

          • Supervise administrative and logistic operations within the battalion, to include development of the
                battalion's combat and field train’s concept and sustainment plan.

          • Collaborate and communicate with their counterparts on the maneuver battalion, BCT, DIVARTY,
                and FAB staffs as required.

          • Direct development of continuity of the operation plan (OPLAN) for the CP, the various operations
                centers, and each staff section.

          • Direct development of mutual support unit operations, as necessary or as directed, with another FA
                unit.

          • Enforce the commander's standards throughout the unit.

     1-68. The battalion commander decides which function (second in command or sustainment of the battalion)
     the XO will primarily focus on any given time. The commander bases this decision on the immediate
     requirements of the battalion and the overall tactical situation. Changing situations will require periodic
     changes in the XO's focus. Their primary function is to understand and executing the battalion commander's
     intent. When the XO is functioning primarily as second in command, one or more of the primary staff officers
     (often the S-4) assumes a major portion of the functions of senior sustainment coordinator. When the XO is
     primarily concerned with the sustainment of the battalion, the S-3 assumes a greater portion of the
     commander's duties in the battalion area.

S-1

     1-69. The S-1 is the coordinating staff officer for all matters concerning human resources support (military
     and civilian). The S-1 provides technical direction to FA battalion units in the areas of: Manning the Force
     (personnel readiness management, personnel accounting, strength reporting; personal information
     management, and casualty operations); Provide Human Resources Services (postal operations and essential
     personnel services); Human Resources Planning and Operations; and Coordinate Personnel Support (morale,
     welfare, and recreation operations, command interest programs, and retention). For more information on the
     duties and responsibilities of the S-1, see FM 1.

S-2

     1-70. The S-2 performs a wide variety of tasks concerning intelligence, targeting, and protection. The S-2
     has specific responsibilities, which include:

          • Supervise the intelligence section.
          • Develop artillery focused IPOE in conjunction with other staff elements, the brigade S-2, and the S-

                2 for any supporting FA units.
          • Develop enemy artillery order of battle and monitor tactics and techniques of enemy artillery,

                mortars, and target acquisition assets.

12  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                               Field Artillery Battalion Organizational Framework

          • Predict enemy artillery target locations in conjunction with the battalion targeting officer and pass
                predicted locations to the appropriate FSE, fires element, FDC, or targeting cell.

          • Provide survivability and mobility information to the battalion S-3.
          • Assess the enemy's capability to interfere with signal communications and supervise the

                counterintelligence aspects of signal operations within the battalion, in conjunction with the S-6.
          • Recommend commander's critical information requirements (CCIRs), especially priority

                intelligence requirements, related to the primary mission, tasks, and role of the battalion.
          • Prepare intelligence estimates, the enemy situation portion of the FA battalion OPLAN or OPORD

                and assists the targeting officer and the target acquisition platoon leader with the target acquisition
                tab of the FA battalion OPORD/FASP, including the radar deployment order, as required.
          • Organize the collection effort designed to answer priority intelligence requirements, in coordination
                with other S-2s.
          • Advise, in conjunction with the battalion targeting officer, the S-3 in positioning, tasking, and
                supervising organic and attached target acquisition assets and coordinating survey for target
                acquisition assets.
          • Assist in developing and collecting the intelligence and target acquisition data necessary to support
                counterfire operations.
          • Assist the counterfire operations section of the FA battalion or the target processing section of the
                DIVARTY or FAB in radar employment and positioning, decision points for cueing (to include
                cueing schedules), radar positioning, and radar zones.
          • Template potential enemy locations; determine enemy-to-friendly FA force ratios; evaluate enemy
                FA target acquisition capabilities and tactics; and advise the FA battalion and maneuver
                commanders on the enemy indirect fire and counterfire threats.
          • Verify IPOE analysis includes evaluation of the role and capabilities of ground, air, and naval forces,
                and possibly satellite and space-based assets in the enemy's counterfire program.
          • Verify subordinate and supporting units are kept informed of the enemy situation.
          • Provide analysis of the air and ground threat to assist the S-3 in coordinating the battalion ground
                and air defense plans.
          • Advise the commander and staff on control of classified materials.
          • Identify the role of intelligence in the development of essential field artillery tasks (EFATs) that
                support essential fire support tasks (EFSTs).

S-3

     1-71. The S-3 is responsible for training, planning, and execution of battalion operations. The S-3 advises
     the battalion commander and has specific responsibilities, which include:

          • FA organization for combat and attack guidance.
          • Identification of EFATs.
          • Positioning of battalion C2 and logistic assets to include CPs, FSC operations, and battalion trains.
          • Direct preparation and dissemination of the FA battalion estimate, MDMP, and OPLAN or

                OPORD/FASP.
          • Employment of organic, attached, and R firing units.
          • Employment of organic and attached survey assets.
          • Logistic priorities in coordination with the XO, S-4, and S-1.
          • Supervise rehearsals.
          • Direct the operations and positioning of the CP.
          • Direct tactical movement, to include selection of positions, preparation of the tactical movement

                plan, and terrain management, for elements under the battalion's control or direction.
          • Direct battalion survey operations.
          • Coordinate plans with higher, subordinate, and adjacent FA units.
          • Verify databases and commander's guidance in automated equipment are correct and properly

                coordinated within the battalion.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  13


---

Chapter 1

          • Plan and manage ammunition consumption, distribution, and resupply, to include calculating
                ammunition requirements, basic loads, required supply rate (RSR), and controlled supply rate
                (CSR), in coordination with the S-4 and FSC commander.

          • Integrate operations security into the overall operations of the unit.
          • Establish communications priorities in conjunction with the S-6.
          • Verify the CP and firing battery leaders understand the priorities, mission routing, clearance of fires,

                and attack guidance within the battalion's zone of fire.
          • Maintain close coordination with the supported higher HQ FSE and other artillery HQ to ensure the

                battalion's responsibilities are coordinated and synchronized, and higher and supported unit
                requirements do not conflict with the FA battalion.
          • Identify detailed responsibilities, to include specified and implied tasks, and FA responsibilities
                during the planning process.
          • Verify ammunition requirements.
          • Evaluate assigned CSRs to determine the impact on counterfire responsibilities.

Targeting Officer

     1-72. The targeting officer and the S-2 section operate as part of the FA battalion counterfire operations
     section within the battalion CP. The targeting officer also serves as the battalion counterfire officer and
     functions as an assistant S-2. The targeting officer focuses on target information received from target
     acquisition sources and uses the Advanced Field Artillery Tactical Data System (AFATDS), distributed
     common ground/surface system-Army, and the effects management tool to collect, analyze and process this
     information into required target guidance and instructions. The targeting officer has specific responsibilities,
     which may include:

          • Assist the S-2 and S-3 in target production, processing, administration, and assist with external
                targeting coordination.

          • Assist the S-2 with enemy FA order of battle development and IPOE.
          • Assist the S-2 with the intelligence, target acquisition, and surveillance portions of the OPLAN or

                OPORD, to include the radar deployment order, as required.
          • Direct attached, organic, and OPCON target acquisition assets.
          • Assist the battalion S-2 and S-3 with development and execution of the target acquisition and

                counterfire plans.
          • Assist the S-2 in developing PIR to support the CCIR.
          • Supervise the intelligence section in the absence of the S-2.
          • Perform officer in charge shift duties for the S-2 section.
          • Update target acquisition coverage of FA target acquisition assets, and position areas for FA target

                acquisition assets.
          • Orient FA target acquisition assets to ensure required coverage.
          • Track the status of FA target acquisition assets.
          • Maintain situational understanding of targeting operations.
          • Recommend target selection standards.
          • Use target selection standards to develop enemy artillery targets and suspect targets.
          • Provide target location error information on available target acquisition assets to the S-2, S-3, and

                brigade FSO as a basis for target selection standards recommendations to the targeting team.
          • Advise the FA battalion commander and the supported higher HQ FSO on counterfire operations.
          • Support targeting meetings, as required.
          • Conduct predictive battle damage assessment and request battle damage assessment from external

                sources and supported higher HQ.

S-4

     1-73. The S-4 coordinates and manages sustainment support for the battalion. The S-4 and FSC commander
     plan the continuity and integration of logistics. The FSC commander and S-4 host periodic logistics
     synchronization meetings with commanders, XOs, and 1SGs to adjust the concept of support and to update
     logistics requirements, as required. The S-4 has specific responsibilities, which include:

14  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                               Field Artillery Battalion Organizational Framework

          • Developing and synchronizing sustainment plans (supply, transportation [tactical and non-tactical],
                maintenance, and services).

          • Plan, prepare, direct, and monitor battalion supply and sustainment operations for all classes of
                supply (except CL VIII).

          • Prepare logistic estimates and plans using logistic preparation of the battlefield methodology and
                develop running estimates.

          • Monitors field feeding, property book activities, maintenance operations, unit basic loads,
                equipment operational status, and the status of requisitions for equipment and supplies for the
                battalion.

          • Monitor ammunition estimates, distribution, and resupply operations, to include authorized basic
                load, CSR, and RSR in coordination with the BN S-3 and FSC commander. Please refer to ATP 4-
                35 for additional information on RSR, CSR, and ammunition.

          • Responsible for developing paragraph 4 of the operation order to include annex F, appendix 1, all
                tabs and exhibits, for attachment to the operation order as required

          • Coordinates with the BSB support operations officer to establish and maintain logistics common
                operating procedures.

          • Managing the property book and maintenance operations to ensure command supply discipline
                program command maintenance discipline program are integrated.

          • Monitors route status to include main and alternate supply routes, bridges, and other sustainment
                and transportation restrictions in coordination with the S-3.

          • Manages and assists with unit movement activities like document preparation; unit movement
                personnel duties; vehicle, aircraft, and railcar load plans

          • Review the battalion's EFATs for critical logistic requirements.

S-6

     1-74. The S-6 is responsible for communications and automation operations, management, and security. The
     S-6 is a coordinating staff officer and is directly accountable to the XO. The S-6 has specific responsibilities,
     which include:

          • Selection of unit position area from a communications standpoint.
          • Communications and automation planning, operations, priorities, security, training, and rehearsals.
          • Communications and automation requirements associated with EFATs (for example, unique

                communications and automation equipment, networks, database exchange, procedures for data
                links, or other critical communications).
          • Direct communications operations to include establishment of communications systems and
                installation and maintenance of equipment.
          • Coordinate integration of battalion communication systems into those of the supported higher HQ,
                maneuver units, subordinate and supporting FA units.
          • Coordinate with signal units for communications support.
          • Supervise operator maintenance of communication equipment.
          • Manage frequency allocations and assignments.
          • Direct COMSEC.
          • Supervise the COMSEC custodian who issues and accounts for COMSEC equipment, key lists,
                codes, ciphers, signal operating instructions (SOI), and authentication systems.
          • Direct automation systems administration, maintenance, and security.
          • Establish automation systems administrative and security procedures for automation hardware and
                software.
          • Direct battalion local area networks configuration and usage of network capabilities.
          • Update global positioning system satellite constellation (ephemeris) data for precision munitions
                capable units.
          • Prepare communications estimates and the signal paragraph of the FA battalion OPORD/FASP.
          • Perform communications reconnaissance and survey to assist the S-3 in positioning key elements
                (for example, retransmission stations) of the battalion.
          • Plan for retransmission sites, as necessary.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  15


---

Chapter 1

Battalion Chaplain

     1-75. The chaplain serves the commander as the personal staff officer to plan, synchronize, coordinate, and
     provide personally delivered religious support. The chaplain advises the commander on moral and ethical
     issues. The chaplain develops a religious support plan, conducts field services, and manages Soldier welfare
     ministries. The chaplain provides counseling to Soldiers and helps maintain the morale and spiritual well-
     being of personnel, as required. A religious affairs specialist supports the chaplain by performing religious
     support duties and coordinating religious programs, worship, and crisis intervention. For more information
     on duties and responsibilities of unit chaplains see ATP 1-05.01.

Forward Support Company Commander

     1-76. The FSC commander should be involved in the planning process to assist the S-4 in determining
     logistics requirements and integrating the concept of sustainment into the tactical plan. The FSC commander
     knows best how to employ the FSC in support of the tactical plan. For more information on the duties and
     responsibilities of a FSC commander see ATP 4-90.

BATTALION S-3 SECTION

     1-77. The battalion S-3 section consists of an assistant S-3, operations SGT, battalion master gunner, CBRN
     officer and NCO, and a battalion ammunition officer. The section performs their duties as directed by the S-
     3.

Assistant S-3

     1-78. The assistant S-3 supervises the activities of the operations section and manages CP operations in the
     absence of the S-3. During operations, the assistant S-3 is usually on shift as a CP battle captain. The assistant
     S-3 constructs the field artillery support matrix (FASM) and positioning overlay for the FA battalion
     OPORD/FASP. During execution, the assistant S-3 ensures that all digital displays and manual status boards
     are updated as necessary; verifies targets and advises the S-3 of targets that violate FSCMs or maneuver
     control measures, or conflict with an ACM. The assistant S-3 focus is on positioning and ranging targets
     established in the FS plan, maintaining howitzer strength, and coordinating ammunition resupply.

Operations Sergeant

     1-79. The operations SGT supervises the activities of enlisted Soldiers in the CP and assists the assistant S-
     3 with operations functions. The operations SGT trains all personnel on CP digital functions. Supervises
     network control duties for the battalion command network. The operations SGT maintains the staff journal
     and message log and verifies all situation maps and graphics, or charts (digital and paper) are always current.
     The operations SGT prepares the briefing site for the battalion's MDMP. The operations SGT verifies the CP
     supplies, maintenance, and security.

Field Artillery Master Gunner

     1-80. The FA Master Gunner is a generalized term used for individuals that have been institutionally trained
     in advanced skills within the FA branch of the of the U.S. Army. Each master gunner provides an essential
     skillset within FA units. They integrate equipment and task-based mastery with advanced training techniques
     and advise commanders on unit training. Operationally, each master gunner is the commander and staff's
     enlisted subject matter expert (SME) within the scope of their equipment specialty.

     1-81. A master gunner is the primary advisor and manager of the commander's certification and qualification
     program. A master gunner is the commander's key resource, and the unit focal point, for administering the
     training and qualification programs IAW TC 3-09.8. Additionally, each master gunner demonstrates
     expertise in the execution of Unit Training Management and related systems such as: Army Training
     Network, Digital Training Management System, and the Combined Arms Training Strategies (CATS)
     Development Tool.

     1-82. The FA master gunner team is comprised of five distinct technical and tactical mastery specialties:
          • Cannon Fires Master Gunner (13B).

16  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026

          • Launcher Fires Master Gunner (13M).
          • Fire Control Master Gunner (13J).
          • Fire Support Master Gunner (13F).
          • Counterfire Master Gunner (13R) for more information on the counterfire master gunner see ATP

                3-09.90.

     1-83. Inherent duties for all FA master gunners with the respective MOS include:
          • Advises the command team on the turnover of key personnel and manning shortfalls at all echelons.
          • Analyzes and records current technical and tactical trends in the field.
          • Advises the CSM on talent management for all personnel; ensuring personnel are in the proper
                positions.
          • Assists the operations officer/operations NCO in planning, resourcing, and managing the unit
                operations.
          • Coordinates with survey team to provide survey support.
          • Provides technical expertise during troubleshooting MOS specific equipment.
          • Provides quality assurance and quality control supervision for all certification and qualification
                events, to include training.
          • Manages certification and qualification records for individuals, teams, crews, sections, platoons,
                batteries, and battalions.
          • Monitors unit status reports and all mission essential equipment, assisting the maintenance process
                as needed.
          • Provides input into the unit SOP.
          • Assists in the forecasting and management of ammunition for certification qualification.
          • Serves as the SME for individual tasks.
          • Manages and assists in planning simulations training.
          • Organizes, or conducts, initial skills training for new personnel.
          • Participates in the MDMP process, providing SME level guidance into the planning process.

Cannon Fires Master Gunner

     1-84. The cannon fires master gunner possesses a multitude of skills, knowledge, and attributes that enable
     success in the Fires warfighting function. The cannon fires master gunner provides an expansion on the
     knowledge, capabilities, and understanding of the technical and tactical aspects of all cannon weapon
     systems. The duties and responsibilities of the cannon fires master gunner are:

          • Operates as the SME for all aspects of cannon weapon systems.
          • Is responsible for the coordination and conduct of survey operations.
          • Assists the command team during the execution of any firing incident investigations.
          • Provides oversight and ensures uniformity within battery/platoon firing positions, as well as

                ammunition sections.
          • Operates as the SME to manage small arms and crew served weapon certification programs.

Launcher Fires Master Gunner

     1-85. The launcher fires master gunner specializes on those FA platforms that typically engage deep area
     targets and beyond. Because of this, the launcher fires master gunner possesses a multitude of skills,
     knowledge, and attributes that produce success in all matters of rocket and missile operations. This position
     provides purposeful expansion of knowledge, capability, and understanding of FA certification and
     qualification at the battalion level. The duties and responsibilities of the launcher fires master gunner are:

          • Operates as the SME for all aspects of rocket and missile weapon systems.
          • Assists the command team during the execution of any firing incident investigations.
          • Assists the CDR and staff in conducting the MDMP process, providing SME level guidance into the

                planning process.
          • Provides oversight and ensures uniformity within battery/platoon firing positions, as well as

                ammunition sections.
          • Operates as the SME to manage small arms and crew served weapon certification programs.

Fire Control Master Gunner

     1-86. The fire control master gunner possesses a multitude of skills, knowledge, and attributes that enable
     success in the Fires warfighting function. The fire control master gunner provides an expansion on the
     knowledge, capabilities, and understanding of the technical and tactical aspects of fire control. The fire
     control master gunner has been institutionally educated to execute these duties and is authorized formal
     approval to exercise certain responsibilities. The duties and responsibilities of the fire control master gunner
     are:

          • Serves as the senior enlisted advisor to the commander and the SME for the CSM on all fire control
                related matters.

          • Assists the command team during the execution of any firing incident investigations.
          • Develops and conducts training of all FA FDOs, fire control NCOs, fire control SGTs, and fire

                control specialists.
          • Assists the operations officer and NCO in planning, resourcing, and managing the battalion

                certification program.
          • Oversees platoon and battery muzzle velocity variation records and logs.
          • Manages the combined muzzle velocity variation record for the battalion.
          • Operates as the SME for FA information system integration into the digital network.
          • Oversees maintenance of primary and secondary computer systems.
          • Operates as the SME to manage small arms and crew served weapon certification programs.
          • Integrates digital communications and database elements with users of non-FA digital C2 systems.

Fire Support Master Gunner

     1-87. The FS master gunner possesses a multitude of skills, knowledge, and attributes that enable success in
     Joint FS. The FS master gunner functions as the Joint FS senior enlisted advisor and master trainer to the
     FSO, FSCOORD, and commander. In an operational environment, the FS master gunner should position
     themselves to advise the FSO, FSCOORD and commander on all matters that pertain to joint FS planning,
     coordination and execution to include how to best employ joint FS personnel and targeting sensors.

     1-88. The duties and responsibilities of the FS master gunner are:
          • Serves as the FA battalion SME in all matters of joint FS sensor systems to include:
                 Training, maintenance, and operational employment of all joint FS personnel, their mounted
                     and dismounted targeting systems, and reporting their status to the FSCOORD and DIVARTY
                     FS master gunner.
                 Training, maintenance, and operational employment of joint FS digital and voice
                     communications equipment and the reporting of their status to the FSCOORD and DIVARTY
                     FS master gunner.
                 Plans, coordinates, executes, and maintains records of the FA battalion/BCT FS qualification
                     Tables I-VI.
                 Assists maintenance personnel with understanding and identifying the components of the FS
                     vehicles mission equipment package.
                 Coordinates with the BSB electronics communications maintenance shop to ensure that regular
                     maintenance is conducted on mounted sensor systems.
          • Serve as the unit JFO program manager to include:
                 Track and report training and qualification status of all JFOs in the BCT FIST to the FSCOORD
                     and DIVARTY FS master gunner.
                 Track and report evaluation status of all JFOs in the BCT FIST to the FSCOORD and
                     DIVARTY FS master gunner.
                 Track and maintain records of applicable JFO, JFO-evaluator, JFO-instructor, primary terminal
                     attack controller, and academic instructors.
                 Ensure that evaluation folders meet the requirements of unit SOP and the most current JFO
                     memorandum of agreement.
                 Assist the DIVARTY FS master gunner with the JFO-evaluator upgrade program.
                 Maintain communication with supporting air support operations squadron and air liaison
                     officer.

Chemical, Biological, Radiological, and Nuclear Officer and Noncommissioned Officer

     1-89. The CBRN officer's roles and responsibilities include the following:
          • Advises the commander on all CBRN threats and hazards, ranging from weapons of mass
                destruction to toxic industrial materials.
          • Develops CBRN defense plan.
          • Implements mission oriented protective posture (referred to as MOPP).
          • Implements CBRN warning and reporting.
          • Prepares CBRN plans and orders.
          • Plans operational decontamination missions.
          • Serves as a battle captain.

     1-90. The CBRN NCO's roles and responsibilities include the following:
          • Advises the commander on the conduct of operations in CBRN environments.
          • Advises the commander on unit CBRN readiness and associated assessments.
          • Advises the commander on the integration of simulated CBRN threats and hazards into unit level
                training and associated assessments.
          • Maintains the unit CBRN room.
          • Maintains appropriate and current publications associated with CBRN operations.
          • Supervises and performs field-level maintenance on unit CBRN defense equipment.
          • Coordinates unit supply activities associated with CBRN defense equipment.
          • Maintains the unit level optical inserts program.
          • Develops, in conjunction with unit leaders, the CBRN portion of unit level administrative,
                deployment, and tactical SOPs as appropriate.
          • Manages unit reports related to CBRN operations.
          • Writes the CBRN portion of the FA battalion OPORD/FASP.
          • Advises the commander on CBRN equipment maintenance, CBRN reconnaissance and surveillance,
                CBRN protection and decontamination, and supports contingency requirements.

     1-91. For more information about core CBRN staff responsibilities and the development of a CBRN annex
     see ATP 3-11.36.

Battalion Ammunition Officer (MLRS/HIMARS)

     1-92. The battalion ammunition officer (rocket battalion) manages re-supply of ammunition in the
     MLRS/HIMARS FA battalion. As such, the battalion ammunition officer may collocate with the operations
     section or in the combat trains CP. The ammunition officer has specific responsibilities, which include:

          • Manage the use of the battalion ammunition carrying assets.
          • Manage ammunition movement from the FSC holding area to the combat trains area and then

                forward to the batteries.
          • Maintain accountability of ammunition.
          • Verify ammunition loads delivered to the batteries contain the proper mix and quantities of

                ammunition.
          • Perform mission analysis to verify ammunition-handling capabilities can support current operations

                and report shortcomings to the S-3.
          • Coordinate with the S-4 on ammunition resupply actions.
          • Manage turn-in of residue and unexpended ammunition.
          • Assist the S-4 with the development and processing of requests for ammunition.

             Note. FA cannon battalions do not have a battalion ammunition officer. The above listed tasks
             and considerations are conducted by the cannon battalion S-4.

BATTALION S-2 SECTION

     1-93. The battalion S-2 section provides tactical focus and targeting capability for the FA battalion.

Chapter 1

Intelligence Sergeant

     1-94. The intelligence SGT assists the S-2 in the planning, supervision, and execution of intelligence and
     targeting operations. The intelligence SGT may be a shift leader for the section, especially in the absence of
     the S-2. In the FA battalion, the intelligence SGT may perform many of the section targeting functions.

TARGET ACQUISITION PLATOON

     1-95. The target acquisition platoon has two key leaders the platoon leader and the target acquisition platoon
     SGT. A description of their duties follows.

Target Acquisition Platoon Leader

     1-96. The platoon leader is responsible for the training, readiness, and maintenance of the platoon WLR
     equipment. The platoon leader supervises the movement, positioning, and emplacement of the WLRs. The
     platoon leader develops the platoon into an effective fighting force capable of performing its combat mission.
     Other duties include:

          • Performs necessary tactical and technical coordination for FA WLRs and data collection systems,
                including communications, encryption, security, force protection, positioning, logistics, and
                administration.

          • Maintains a status of FA WLRs and informs the commander and counterfire officer when necessary.
                Advises the commander and staff on the technical considerations affecting the employment of
                WLRs and recommends the general locations of radar sites and search azimuths.

          • Monitors the mission support requirements of all WLRs within the supported area.
          • Assists in the selection of radar sites.
          • Reviews and consolidates requisitions for tools, repair parts, supplies, and equipment.
          • Inspects maintenance of platoon vehicles and equipment.
          • Monitors the terrain management plan for the positioning of each WLR section.
          • Maintains communications between the section, battalion and the counterfire HQ.
          • Performs necessary tactical coordination for WLRs in the area of operations (AO).
          • Assists in the development of the radar deployment order using the DA Form 5957 (Radar

                Deployment Order [LRA]).
          • Responsible for the certification and qualification of the WLR sections.

Target Acquisition Platoon Sergeant

     1-97. The platoon SGT is the senior enlisted advisor to the platoon leader and must be prepared to assume
     the duties and responsibilities of the platoon leader. The platoon SGT ensures that WLR personnel are trained
     to perform all procedures used during combat operations. The platoon SGT is responsible for the
     maintenance, logistics, and discipline of the platoon. The platoon SGT works in close coordination with the
     platoon leader to ensure unity of effort. Other responsibilities consist of the following:

          • Mentors and evaluates each WLR section chief.
          • Coordinates survey support for the platoon.
          • Coordinates and recommends general PAA and search areas in accordance with the commander's

                guidance and intent.
          • Supervises maintenance and training of the WLR sections.
          • Participates in the development of radar deployment order.
          • Maintains the WLR maintenance status chart.
          • Manages all administrative actions for the radar platoon in conjunction with the counterfire NCO

                and section chief.
          • Prepares and maintains accountability charts for all essential repair parts stockage list.
          • Coordinates higher-level maintenance for unserviceable equipment.
          • Performs survey support to the battalion and supported units.

BATTALION FIRE DIRECTION CENTER

     1-98. The FDC has two key leaders the FDO and the senior fire control SGT. A description of their duties
     follows.
