                                                 Chapter 2

         Field Artillery Battalion Command and Control

    Section I describes command and support relationships. Section II describes the FA battalion
    CP and its operation. Section III discusses FA battalion communications.

SECTION I – COMMAND AND SUPPORT RELATIONSHIPS

     2-1. FA cannon, rocket, and missile systems that are organic, assigned, attached to, or under the OPCON of
     FABs, DIVARTY, and BCTs provide commanders with continuously available, adjustable, and tailored FS
     under all weather conditions and in all types of terrain. These organizations can be task-organized with
     maneuver or a supported commander to provide the right combination of indirect fires capabilities for a given
     situation. See FM 3-09 for additional information on FA command and support relationships.

COMMAND AND SUPPORT RELATIONSHIPS

     2-2. C2 of an FA battalion is established through a two-step process. The first step is to establish a command
     relationship with a senior HQ. This relationship - either organic, assigned, attached, or OPCON - is normally
     established with an FA HQ, such as DIVARTY or FAB. However, it can be established with a maneuver or
     other supported HQ.

     2-3. Once a command relationship is established, the second step is to assign a support relationship. The four
     standard FA support relationships are DS, R, GSR, and general support (GS). The assignment of a support
     relationship and the inherent responsibilities associated with each mission establish in detail the relationship
     between the supporting FA unit and the maneuver or FA headquarter being supported.

DIRECT SUPPORT

     2-4. Direct support is a support relationship requiring a force to support another specific force and
     authorizing it to answer directly to the supported force's request for assistance (FM 3-0). A FA battalion
     operating in DS (typically cannon units) of a unit is primarily concerned with the FA support needs of only
     that unit. The DS battalion commander is the FSCOORD for the supported maneuver force, normally a
     maneuver brigade. Fires are planned and coordinated with the maneuver unit, and the battalion is positioned
     where it can best support the scheme of maneuver. If the battalion cannot provide the support required for a
     planned scheme of maneuver, it is the responsibility of the FSCOORD to make this known to the supported
     commander. The same battalion should habitually support the same maneuver or supported force to provide
     continuity.

     2-5. Each DS battalion trains and deploys four FS sections and one FIST HQ section. It sends one FS section
     to the designated maneuver brigade. After initial deployment, FS sections and FISTs will remain with the
     maneuver unit throughout the conflict.

     2-6. The responsibility for immediate replacements for casualty losses to FS section and FIST personnel and
     equipment rests with the FA battalion with the DS mission. Similarly supply and maintenance support should
     be specified in terms of what the supported unit will provide and what the parent battalion will provide.

REINFORCING

     2-7. Reinforcing is a support relationship requiring a force to support another supporting unit (FM 3-0). FA
     battalions reinforce other FA battalions that support maneuver or other supported forces. An R support
     relationship requires one FA unit to augment the fires of another FA unit.

     2-8. When an organic or DS FA battalion requires more fires to meet supported force requirements, another
     FA battalion may be directed to reinforce it. An organic or DS FA battalion may be supported by up to 2 R
     battalions. A FA unit assigned an R support relationship retains its command relationship with its parent unit
     but is positioned by the reinforced FA unit and has priorities of support established by the reinforced FA unit,
     then the parent unit.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  23


---

Chapter 2

GENERAL SUPPORT REINFORCING

     2-9. The GSR mission requires the FA battalion to furnish artillery fires for the force as a whole and to
     reinforce the fires of another FA battalion as a second priority. A GSR battalion remains under the control of
     the force field artillery HQ, which has priority of fires. However, a quick-fire channel is established for
     immediate response to the reinforced battalion's need for additional fires. The GSR mission offers the force
     commander flexibility to meet the requirements of a variety of tactical situations.

GENERAL SUPPORT

     2-10. A FA battalion assigned the mission of GS provides FS for the force as a whole and remains under the
     immediate control of the force field artillery HQ. The GS mission provides immediate FA indirect fires
     responsive to the needs of the force commander. The GS mission is the most centralized of the support
     relationships.

     2-11. Each FA support relationship provides the following FA support responsibilities:
          • From whom and in what priority does the unit answer calls for fire?
          • What is the zone of fire of the unit?
          • Who furnishes FIST or FSO?
          • With whom does the unit establish liaison?
          • With whom does the unit establish communications?
          • Who positions the unit?
          • Who plans the fires of the unit?

     2-12. FA battalions' best meet their FS responsibilities through utilization of the four support relationships.
     Referred to as DS, R, GSR, and GS. FA personnel must know the seven FA inherent responsibilities in Army
     support relationships listed in FM 3-09. Each support relationship specifies the way of meeting each of the
     seven inherent responsibilities. The support relationships and their responsibilities are summarized in Table
     2-1.

         Table 2-1. Support relationships and responsibilities of the field artillery battalion

    All FA units with a     Direct Support      Reinforcing       General Support        General Support
         mission of:                                                 Reinforcing

     Answers calls for      1. Supported Unit   1. Reinforced FA  1. Force FA HQ         1. Force FA HQ
    fire in priority from:  2. Own Observers    2. Own Observers  2. Own Observers       2. Own Observers
                            3. Force FA HQ      3. Force FA HQ    3. Reinforced Unit

    Has as its zone of      Zone of action of   Zone of fire of   Zone of action of      Zone of action of
    fire:                   supported unit      reinforced FA     supported unit to      supported unit
                                                                  include zone of fire
                                                                  of reinforced FA unit

    Furnishes               Provides temporary  No requirement    No requirement         No requirement
    FIST/FSE2               replacements of
                            casualty losses as
                            required

    Furnishes liaison       No requirement      LNO to reinforced FA LNO to reinforced FA LNO to supported
    officer:
                                                unit HQ           unit HQ                unit HQ

    FA – field artillery, FIST – fire support team, FSE – fire support element, HQ – headquarters, LNO – liaison officer

NONSTANDARD MISSIONS

     2-13. In tactical situations where the commander's intent cannot be accurately conveyed with one of the
     support relationships, a nonstandard mission may be assigned. Nonstandard missions amplify, limit, or
     change one or more of the seven inherent responsibilities or spell out contingencies not covered by the seven
     inherent responsibilities.

     2-14. If the revision of a mission is so complex that the standard support relationship is no longer
     recognizable, a nonstandard mission statement will be issued that specifically addresses each of the seven
     inherent responsibilities or other areas not covered by the seven inherent responsibilities. An example of a
     nonstandard FA support relationship includes:

24                                              ATP 3-09.23                              25 February 2026


---

                                                                                    Field Artillery Battalion Command and Control

          • 110 FAB: GSR 7 FAB; do not exceed 25 percent of the CSR in support of 7 FAB.
          • 1-89 FA (MLRS, M270A1): GS; provide LNO to the corps FSE.

     2-15. Commanders and staffs must consider the principles of FS execution referred to as the memory aid
     AWIFM-N when planning the integration of fires into an operation see FM 3-09. FS execution principles
     AWIFM-N considerations are:

          • Adequate FS for the committed units.
          • Weight to the main effort.
          • Immediately available FS for the commander to influence the operations.
          • Facilitate future operations.
          • Maximum feasible centralized control (especially in the defense).
          • Never place artillery in reserve.

     2-16. In addition to never leaving FA in reserve and the FS execution principles of AWIFM-N, FA
     commanders and FS personnel also consider mission, ammunition, and positioning when planning for FA
     system employment. Considerations include:

          • Mission. Appropriate FA unit command support relationships assigned based on the commander's
                intent and the overall situation.

          • Ammunition. Allocate ammunition to provide more support for the main effort. Ensure adequate
                ammunition for future operations through positioning, ammunition restrictions, or CSR parameters
                during execution and planning.

          • Positioning. Continually re-position FA units, ammunition, target acquisition resources, CPs, and
                assets to best support the plan as it is being executed. Assign direction of fire to mass fires in the
                appropriate AO.

     2-17. Augmentation with additional FA units depends upon the mission variables of METT-TC (I), which
     may include:

          • A FA battalion with a support relationship. For example, the division or corps commander may
                place an augmenting FAB or BCT FA battalion in a R or GSR relationship to a BCT's organic FA
                battalion.

          • A FA battalion given a different command relationship. For example, an augmenting FA battalion
                attached to a BCT. The brigade commander has the authority to:
                 Give the FA battalion a R support relationship to an organic FA battalion.
                 Attach the FA unit to the organic FA battalion.

          • United States Marine Corps or Multinational artillery units may support a BCT. Command, support,
                and liaison requirements must be coordinated. United States Marine Corps artillery units and North
                Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) artillery units are prepared to accomplish the tactical mission.

     2-18. If the FA battalion is operating as part of a unified action, several other command relationship terms
     may be encountered (combatant command, operational command) during support. Detailed information on
     these command relationship terms is in FM 3-09 and ADP 5-0.

     2-19. The FA battalion commander and staff also need to understand that some of the command relationship
     terms used have slightly different definitions within multinational forces than within Department of Defense.
     For example, in NATO, OPCON does not include authority to assign separate employment of components
     of the units concerned. (The NATO definition of OPCON more closely represents the Department of Defense
     definition of tactical control.) During unified action, FA battalion commanders should seek clarification from
     their higher commander or verify the definitions of operational terms through review of orders or from the
     agreements that established the force. Commanders use agreed-upon multinational command and support
     relationships when controlling multinational forces. For more information on multinational operation see FM
     3-16.

ON-ORDER COMMAND OR SUPPORT RELATIONSHIPS

     2-20. During an operation, a FA battalion may change command or support relationships. The assignment of
     an on-order FA relationship gives the battalion advance notice. This facilitates planning for and transition to
     the follow-on command or support relationship. Positioning, ammunition requirements, and timing of the
     change are several of the key considerations for battalions that have an on-order relationship. An on-order

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  25


---

Chapter 2

     relationship is in the maneuver OPORD, FS plan, and the FA battalion OPLAN or OPORD/FASP. For more
     information see FM 3-09.

SECTION II – COMMAND AND CONTROL

     2-21. FA battalion commander exercises C2 through the establishment of a battalion CP. The CP, which is
     typically comprised of the S-3, S-2, S-6, FDC, and the counterfire operations section, is habitually located
     with the command group. Depending on mission variables, a combat trains CP may be established between
     the brigade support area and FA battalion main CP to provide more flexible and responsive logistical support
     to the battalion. The combat trains CP is the operations center for the combat trains. See Chapter 7.

     2-22. The primary command facility is the battalion CP. The commander positions the command group with
     the CP. It is normally located where the commander can best command assets and influence FA and other
     fires systems in support of operations.

     2-23. The battalion will also designate an alternate CP, which will assume control of battalion operations in
     the event the main CP becomes inoperable or loses communications. The alternate CP may be another FA
     battalion, a firing battery, the supported higher HQ FSE, or the combat train CP. The battalion may also form
     a tactical CP forward during high intensity, fast moving operations. The tactical CP concentrates on the
     current battle, performing critical operations, fire direction, and intelligence tasks, while the main CP
     performs planning and coordination functions.

     2-24. In offensive operations, the main CP may be required to move forward to shorten communications
     distances and maintain control of the firing batteries and platoons. Under these conditions, the FA battalion
     may use a tactical CP technique, moving some elements of the main CP forward while the rear element of
     the main CP maintains control. Once established forward the tactical CP takes control, and the rear element
     breaks down and moves forward to reform a main CP. See CP positioning and movement later in the chapter
     for a discussion of employment of the tactical CP.

COMMAND POST ORGANIZATION AND OPERATION

     2-25. The makeup of the FA battalion CP will vary with the situation and the commander's concept of
     operations. Most of the elements that comprise the CP are in the HHB, which may include:

          • Command group.
          • Main CP.
          • Communications support.
          • Survey elements.

COMMAND GROUP

     2-26. The command group consists of the battalion commander, assisted by the CSM, the XO, and their
     drivers. They frequently operate out of the CP, where they can best monitor and control the battalion's
     operations.

     2-27. The FA battalion commander positions where best to fulfill command responsibilities. This may be in
     the FA battalion CP, a trains location, a firing battery, or a key traffic or observation point. The FA battalion
     commander may at times be in the brigade HQ, a maneuver battalion FSE, or with the brigade commander
     at a critical location on the battlefield. The FA battalion commander will have digital equipment and radios
     in their vehicle that allows them to monitor digital traffic and aid in command of the battalion while operating
     away from the CP.

     2-28. The members of the command group are often away from the CP observing, directing, or otherwise
     influencing the action at critical places on the battlefield. The battalion XO, CSM, and S-3 provide critical
     support, which allows the FA battalion commander the flexibility to be positioned where the situation
     dictates.

     2-29. The battalion XO may operate out of the battalion trains CP instead of the main CP, especially when
     focusing on the battalion's sustainment operations. During periods of major planning actions, or when the

26  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                                    Field Artillery Battalion Command and Control

     commander and S-3 may be out of the main CP for extended periods, the XO will probably be in the main
     CP.

MAIN COMMAND POST

     2-30. The main CP serves as the FA battalion's primary operations center, assisting the battalion commander
     in synchronizing fires in support of brigade operations. It is the location in the battalion where the majority
     of planning, staff coordination, plan execution, receiving and disseminating information, and monitoring of
     key events occur. Critical functions of the CP include:

          • Advise the battalion commander, and as appropriate, key FS personnel, on the organization for
                combat, positioning, allocation of ammunition and attack guidance.

          • Perform tactical fire direction, to include selection of units and ammunition to support fire mission
                requests in response to the supported commander's attack guidance and to create the desired effects.

          • Plan operations, to include generating a FA battalion, OPLAN or OPORD/FASP that outlines the
                concept of operations and responsibilities for the battalion and describes the FA battalion
                commander's plan for accomplishing assigned missions and responsibilities. Note: In the FA
                battalion, the OPLAN or OPORD/FASP must address the scheme of fires and is prepared in
                coordination with the supported higher HQ FSE as part of the maneuver OPORD/FASP. The
                operations section, in coordination with the maneuver FSEs, ensure that the FA plan is synchronized
                with the maneuver force plan. The CP automation systems provide a collaborative planning
                capability to assist in these planning functions.

          • Direct current operations, to include controlling fires and target acquisition assets that are organic,
                attached, or R to the battalion; move and position firing elements; orchestrate the delivery of
                effective fires in support of close combat.

          • Monitor technical fire direction, to include providing technical assistance to battery and platoon
                FDCs.

          • Maintain situational understanding of the overall supported higher HQ operation to ensure the
                battalion provides timely, responsive support and rapidly adjusts to any changes encountered.

          • Conduct information management operations, receiving, processing, and disseminating critical
                battlefield information in all formats.

          • Direct counterfire operations.
          • Conduct essential intelligence operations and tasks.
          • Perform targeting.
          • Direct and coordinate tactical unmanned aircraft system (UAS) employment.
          • Advise the S-4 of Class V requirements.
          • Direct survey operations to support the battalion's operations.
          • Direct communications operations, to include radio, wire, automation management, local area

                networks, and signal security.
          • Direct battalion operations within a CBRN environment to include CBRN defense.
          • Provide general direction of administrative and logistic operations in coordination with the battalion

                trains CP, FSC, and the firing batteries.

HEADQUARTERS AND HEADQUARTERS BATTERY COMMANDER AND FIRST SERGEANT

     2-31. The HHB commander and 1SG are responsible for the security and logistical support for the CP.
     Dispersed elements of HHB at the battalion main CP, or at the battalion trains CP, must work closely with
     staff officers and section leaders to ensure adequate support for all elements.

     2-32. With the HHB commander and 1SG usually directing the security, support, and movement of the
     battalion main CP, the FSC commander and 1SG assume responsibility for the FA battalion support area.
     Due to dispersion of personnel, both leadership teams must coordinate their efforts between the CP and the
     FA battalion support area. The FA battalion commander designates command and security responsibilities
     for the battalion trains CP. While positioning and movement of the main CP is primarily an S-3 responsibility,
     the HHB commander and 1SG assist in reconnaissance, movement, and occupation.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  27


---

Chapter 2

COMMUNICATIONS SUPPORT

     2-33. The S-6, S-3, and the HHB commander work together to ensure the CP has adequate communications
     support. This primarily includes assistance in the setup and maintenance of radio, digital communications
     and automation equipment, and retransmission capability. The S-6 supports the battalions other operations
     centers and the firing batteries. The communications element in the CP may consist of a contact team that
     travels with and gives priority to CP support.

SURVEY ELEMENTS

     2-34. Survey for the battalion is provided by an ad-hoc team. The survey team may base their operations out
     of the CP to allow better control by the S-3.

     2-35. There is no longer a military occupational specialty dedicated solely to survey. This creates a need to
     train non-survey soldiers on how to operate the survey equipment. The commander designates the personnel
     to train and form the survey team. The survey team provides survey for the unit. The survey equipment is
     assigned to the FA battalion. For more information on survey see FM 3-09 and ATP 3-09.02.

COMMAND POST ORGANIZATION

     2-36. The operations, intelligence element, and the battalion FDC are major functional elements of the FA
     battalion CP. The operations and intelligence elements manage both current and future operations and
     coordinates aspects of FA support. They also perform planning and operational functions, to include
     developing fire plans, orders, conducting artillery focused IPOE, developing artillery targets, planning, and
     executing supported higher HQ counterfire operations, tracking the status of subordinate units, and
     controlling unit movements.

     2-37. The FDC performs tactical fire direction. This is performed by processing calls for fire, determining
     the type and amount of ammunition required to create the desired effects, and transmitting fire orders to the
     platoon FDCs or platoon operations centers.

S-3 OPERATIONS SECTION

     2-38. The operations section responsibilities include:
          • Plan and coordinate the positioning of logistic elements, firing batteries, and platoons supporting
                operations.
          • Plan and coordinate battalion movements and assist the battery commanders with coordination of
                movements. This includes assignment of routes, PAA, and their clearance through the AO.
          • Maintain current operational status of organic, attached, and R and reinforced units.
          • Prepare and disseminate operational reports.
          • Maintain friendly situational understanding and common operational picture (COP).
          • Coordinate and integrate risk management and other protection tasks into plans and operations.
          • Disseminate the battalion COP.
          • Coordinate survey requirements for the supported higher HQ to include radars or units from the
                FAB or DIVARTY.
          • Advise the FDC, FSEs, R and reinforced units on scheduling of planned fires, as required.
          • Prepare and disseminate the FA battalion OPLAN or OPORD, while assisting FSEs with
                development of the supported higher HQ FS plan.
          • Monitor ammunition consumption and resupply for the battalion.
          • Inform battalion sections like FSC commander, combat trains, S-1, and S-4 of the status of the
                supported forces and any changes to sustainment requirements.
          • Supervise operations in CBRN environment.
          • Direct operations security within the battalion along with the S-2 and S-6.
          • Assume control of R and reinforced artillery battalions during mutual support unit operations, as
                required.
          • Coordinate communications requirements with the S-6 for the battalion.

28  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                                    Field Artillery Battalion Command and Control

S-2 SECTION

     2-39. The S-2 intelligence section provides the commander and S-3 with information and intelligence
     essential to the operation and survival of the battalion. The intelligence section responsibilities include:

          • Prepare artillery focused IPOE products of the supported higher HQ AO in coordination with the
                brigade S-2. The FA battalion IPOE is an extension of the BCT IPOE and higher HQ IPOE, focused
                on specific artillery-related intelligence requirements. The IPOE process is a continuous process.

          • Assist the S-3 in battery and platoon PAA selection to ensure that positions are aligned with IPOE
                insights and survivability requirements.

          • Assist the counterfire operations section in the development of the target acquisition Annex or Tab
                to the FA battalion OPLAN or OPORD and the radar deployment order for organic and attached
                radars. Ensures the radar deployment order designates positions and establishes cueing procedures.
                Coordinate the use of WLRs, organic or attached, with the battalion S-3.

          • Develop targeting data based on the brigade commander's HPT list and attack guidance matrix.
                Provide recommendations and input to the targeting team that develop the HPT list and attack
                guidance matrix for the brigade commander.

          • Monitor enemy artillery tactics and techniques within the supported higher HQ area of operation.
          • Exchange combat information and intelligence with the supported higher HQ, subordinate units, R,

                reinforced units, and adjacent units, as required.
          • Coordinate with battery 1SGs to develop a ground and air defense plan for the battalion.
          • Assist the S-3 with planning, coordination, and conduct of operations security.
          • Coordinate external battalion security requirements.
          • Support the development of CCIR, specifically priority intelligence requirements.

COUNTERFIRE OPERATIONS SECTION

     2-40. The counterfire operations section and the FA battalion S-2 make up the target processing team. This
     is a working group that recommends, and updates target acquisition coverage, control of FA target acquisition
     assets and position areas for FA target acquisition assets. The counterfire operations section duties during the
     conduct of counterfire operations include:

          • Advise the FA battalion commander on counterfire operations.
          • Produce the radar deployment order for the supported higher HQ.
          • Orient FA target acquisition assets to ensure required coverage of the supported higher HQ.
          • Track FA target acquisition assets and reports status to the S-2 and S-3.
          • Assist the battalion S-2 and S-3 with development and execution of the FA target acquisition and

                counterfire plan.
          • Maintain situational understanding of targeting operations.
          • Recommend target selection standards.
          • Use target selection standards to develop enemy artillery targets and suspect targets.
          • Provide target location error information on available target acquisition assets to the S-2, S-3, and

                brigade FSO as a basis for target selection standards recommendation to the targeting working
                group.
          • Support targeting meetings, as required.
          • Assist the battalion S-2 in writing the target acquisition and surveillance portions of the FA battalion
                OPLAN or OPORD/FASP.
          • Direct attached, organic, and OPCON target acquisition assets.
          • Assist the S-2 in developing PIRs.
          • Assist battalion staff to develop commander critical information requirements.
          • Conduct predictive battle damage assessment and request battle damage assessment from external
                sources and supported higher HQ.
          • Assist in the planning of tactical UAS missions and operations.

COMMAND POST ACTIVITIES

     2-41. The S-3 is positioned in the CP to see and hear critical information that will allows making sound
     tactical decisions based on the commander's guidance. In the event the S-3 is not in the CP, the assistant S-3

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  29


---

Chapter 2

     or shift officer assumes the S-3's responsibility of managing CP operations. The S-3 and XO should develop
     shifts to cover 24-hour operations. This should be covered in the tactical SOP.

     2-42. Each section should maintain a shift log, documenting the major events, actions, and message traffic
     applicable to the section, as well as accomplishment of major shift responsibilities. The log serves as a record
     of the major events and as a tool to prepare for shift briefings.

     2-43. Shifts should overlap to allow proper handover of the battle. In addition, shift changeover for each
     section may be staggered to improve continuity. Update approximately an hour before shift change, maps,
     status boards, shift logs, and other tools with current information; filing and document destruction is
     accomplished; and general CP housekeeping completed. The oncoming shift should be given time to review
     maps, status boards, shift logs, and other applicable tools. The outgoing shift should provide a shift briefing
     that address, as a minimum, current operational status, CP status, and battalion CP and section tasks. The S-
     3 or section leaders may establish a tactical SOP format for the shift change briefing.

MANAGEMENT OF TACTICAL INFORMATION

     2-44. The battalion S-3 and the rest of the CP staff control the day-to-day operations of the FA battalion.
     They receive and track battlefield information into tactical movement, delivery of fires, and sustainment
     operations. For the battalion staff to effectively track the tactical situation and satisfy the maneuver
     commander's concept of fires, situational understanding and battle tracking must be as accurate as possible.
     Using digital systems, the staff provides situational understanding, battle tracking capabilities, collaborative
     planning, and rapid dissemination of information.

     2-45. The primary computer system provides automated planning, preparation, execution, and assessment of
     close combat, counterfire, interdiction, shaping operations, and suppression of enemy air defenses (SEAD).
     The primary computer system interfaces with other automation systems. It can also provide numerous
     separate overlays to allow specific information when needed such as enemy overlay, target overlay, friendly
     artillery locations overlay, and operational graphics.

     2-46. Display the commander's tactical display at the FA battalion CP. Display the supported higher HQ
     staffs map, overlays, and other control measures. The primary computer system publishes unit information,
     targets, and certain geometries that other automated systems in the CP can access.

     2-47. Automated systems display the current situation on a map with boundaries and other control measures.
     It is important to remember that the current situation provided by a satellite-based system has a time delay
     and is near-real time, not real time.

     2-48. Where the various systems are located throughout the CP will depend on the CP design as determined
     by the commander, XO, and S-3. It is imperative that each staff member fully understand the capabilities of
     each system and use each system to facilitate CP information management. The physical layout of the CP
     should support the flow of information and allow the S-3 to direct the efforts of the staff in an efficient
     manner.

     2-49. The S-3 is positioned in the CP to effectively manage personnel. The S-3 is positioned to have access
     to radios, telephones, and digital display devices. The S-3 also monitors and manages the back-up systems,
     such as the operations and intelligence maps and other manual status charts.

     2-50. The primary information sources in the CP are its internal and external digital and voice networks. In
     a FA battalion, the networks that give the S-3 the clearest picture of current events on the battlefield are the
     supported higher HQ FS coordination network and the command voice network. It is important to understand
     that the FA battalion staff monitors these networks and does not transmit on them. To reduce confusion in
     the CP, it is recommended these networks be the only ones audible on remote devices in the operations
     (extension) area. Use headsets when monitoring the other voice networks or monitor from within the
     appropriate staff vehicles. The S-3 is kept informed of most of the traffic on the other networks (data and
     voice) by status charts updates, reviewing message forms, and computer displays. Bring anything of
     significance that requires the S-3's attention to the S-3 immediately.

     2-51. The S-3's principal networks for fire planning and execution are the battalion fire direction, operations,
     fires, and supported higher HQ FS networks. These networks allow the CP to review fire missions and
     perform a secondary independent check of FS coordination measures. The S-3 can observe targets in relation

30  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                               Field Artillery Battalion Command and Control

     to the tactical situation. The S-3 must closely monitor the information received from all tactical information
     sources to verify critical data, identify potentially inaccurate information, and to resolve discrepancies
     between conflicting reports or data.

     2-52. By monitoring the supported command network plus the situational understanding provided by
     automation systems, the S-3 determines the tempo of the operation, anticipates where fires will be directed
     by the battalion commander or brigade FSO, and maintains situational awareness. This allows the S-3 to be
     proactive in positioning units and managing ammunition for effective tactical control of fires.

     2-53. The CP passes tactical orders and information to the batteries and any supporting units over the
     battalion command network. Tactical orders and information are sent to the firing platoons over AFATDS.
     The voice command network radio is remoted to the operations SGT's post and monitored with a headset.
     The S-3 receives the information passed on this network by monitoring the computer screens and by
     reviewing messages and reports used to update the situation. Avoid sending situation reports and updates of
     information with battalion combat and field trains on this network. Send routine administrative and logistical
     traffic on the battalion administrative and logistics network.

     2-54. The S-2 section monitors the supported higher HQ operations and intelligence network. The focus of
     the traffic on operations and intelligence network is maneuver specific, but the S-2 can gather information
     relevant to FA support requirements and operational security considerations. The S-2 section should monitor
     this with either a remote (headset) or from inside the S-2 vehicle. Pass routine traffic to the S-3 in message
     form. The S-3 monitors critical traffic requiring attention, on the S-2's remote. The S-2 shares target
     information generated by the target acquisition platoon with the supported higher HQ analyst.

DISPLAY OF INFORMATION

     2-55. Automation systems provide the FA battalion CP with tools for the management of information. The
     digital systems are very dependent on generator power and the primary computer system will not be available
     on the move. It is recommended that the CP also maintain alternate information management tools to augment
     and back-up the digital systems, which may include:

          • Map boards that facilitate exchange of overlays with minimum loss of accuracy.
          • Overlays with tactical information to assist CP personnel in planning and executing operations (for

                example, maneuver graphics, PAA, target, and sustainment).
          • Status boards along functional lines to track the current status of elements and other combat

                information.

COMMAND POST POSITIONING AND MOVEMENT

     2-56. The S-3 controls the movement and positioning of the FA battalion CP based on the guidance and
     direction of the commander. The S-2 provides mobility information concerning terrain, trafficability of roads,
     obstacles, minefields, contaminated areas, and survivability information regarding ground, air, and CBRN
     threats from enemy forces and the operational environment. The S-2 also provides information about the
     civilian populace in the area. The S-6 provides guidance on communications factors. The other members of
     the CP may also assist with reconnaissance, security, and advance party operations. The FSE assists with
     clearance for the positions and movement routes that must be coordinated with the supported higher HQ staff
     and subordinate maneuver battalions.

COMMAND POST POSITIONING CONSIDERATIONS

     2-57. The S-3 uses the IPOE products in determining positions. The S-3 usually plans primary, alternate, and
     any supplementary CP positions. The primary consideration for positioning the CP is the ability to
     accomplish its mission. The S-3 must also consider if the CP will be collocated with other elements, such as
     the battalion trains or a supported unit.

Protection and Survivability of the Command Post

     2-58. Depending on the threat, CPs need to remain as small as possible and highly mobile, especially at lower
     echelons. Considerations for CP survivability include but are not limited to dispersion, size, redundancy,
     mobility, electromagnetic emissions, and signatures see (FM 6-0). Survivability is a quality or capability of
     military forces which permits them to avoid or withstand hostile actions or environmental conditions while

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  31


---

Chapter 2

     retaining the ability to fulfill their primary mission (ATP 3-37.34) Survivability operations are those
     protection activities that alter the physical environment by providing or improving cover, camouflage, and
     concealment (ATP 3-37.34. In other words, a specific group of tasks that enhance survivability.

     2-59. Position the CP based on general movement of the forward line of own troops (FLOT), which may be
     forward, rearward, static, or erratic.

          • In a sustained, rapidly advancing offensive operation, position the CP as far forward as feasible.
          • During a retrograde, place the CP farther to the rear than normal. On a non-linear battlefield,

                increased security considerations may be less predictable than on a linear battlefield.

     2-60. If there is a high enemy counterfire threat, position the CP outside the range of mortars and as many of
     the enemy's artillery systems as feasible. Defilade or reverse slope positioning may provide increased
     protection; however, retransmission of communications may be necessary.

     2-61. In response to high enemy air threat, position the CP in heavily wooded or urban terrain that allows
     better camouflage. Place the CP close to an air and missile defense unit that can provide coverage. The use
     of wooded or urban terrain, coupled with camouflage and light discipline, are also critical to reducing
     vulnerability to air and satellite imagery.

     2-62. Position the CP with or near other friendly elements when there is a high risk from enemy penetrating
     forces or small force operations. When there is a threat of rapid penetration from an enemy attack or
     counterattack, position the CP off the expected axis of advance, especially any high-speed avenues of
     approach. For increased security, position infantry, military police, or logistic elements with the CP.

     2-63. If there is a significant electromagnetic warfare threat, position the CP away from the supported CPs
     and the battalion trains to reduce the threat to them and to reduce the electromagnetic signature. To reduce
     electromagnetic locating vulnerability, position the CP in defilade or masked locations and use
     retransmission. Position the CP close to the maneuver CP when extensive coordination with the BCT HQ is
     critical.

     2-64. Consider communications factors such as digital and radio communication ranges and retransmission
     capability. Communication considerations are the distances between units, the capabilities of the equipment,
     atmospheric conditions, and terrain. Consider grounding of equipment, tie-downs for extensions and shelters,
     and preparation of defensive positions for terrain that is extremely wet, rocky, or steep, and urban terrain that
     may interfere with weapon platforms. Consider hilly or mountainous terrain that provides survivability
     advantages but may also interfere with communications for the CP.

TACTICAL COMMAND POST CONSIDERATIONS

     2-65. To facilitate the continuity of operations during movement of the CP to a new location, the battalion
     may use a tactical CP. This is a variation of movement by echelon. A small portion of the main CP, and
     minimal security and support elements, will move to the new location in advance of the remainder of the CP.
     Designate a FDC to assume control of fire mission processing and act as the battalion until the main CP can
     re-assume control and continue to process fire missions. Maintain tactical fire control at the main CP (-)
     during the tactical CP movement. Once the tactical CP is in place it conducts a continuity of operations
     exchange with the main CP (-) and establishes communications with subordinate, higher, and supported units
     before it assumes control of the battalion. The remainder of the CP then moves to the new location and normal
     operations can resume.

     2-66. Composition of the tactical CP is limited to key equipment and personnel. As an example, a tactical
     CP may include:

          • A high-mobility multipurpose-wheeled vehicle; operations vehicle with extension.
          • A primary computer system, status boards and situation maps.
          • S-2 or S-3; assistant S-3; CP NCO; FDO and FDC NCO; S-2 NCO; selected operations and

                intelligence and FDC personnel; and other HHB elements to provide support and security.

     2-67. The size and composition of the tactical CP will depend on the tactical situation and the S-3's major
     concerns. A smaller tactical CP may be preferred during periods of rapid advancement and frequent moves,
     while utilizing a larger tactical CP if security is a major issue, and speed of movement is not critical. During

32  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                               Field Artillery Battalion Command and Control

     a rapidly advancing offensive operation, the tactical CP may be moving again shortly after the rest of the CP
     has closed and assumed control of the battalion.

     2-68. The unit can use a version of the tactical CP technique in a reversed sequence, during defensive
     operations when the battalion must move to the rear or laterally away from a penetration. The bulk of the CP
     would move first, while the tactical CP remains in place controlling the battalion until a new CP is established.
     This allows quick displacement of the tactical CP and the ability to remain close to the fight.

     2-69. The battalion can also form a tactical CP to place the battalion commander or S-3 closer to the action,
     near the maneuver commander, the battalion CP, or the supported higher HQ FSE. Use the tactical CP during
     operations requiring frequent, fast moves where the main CP would have trouble-keeping pace with the
     supported maneuver unit. The tactical CP would be the focal point for battalion control of the current fight,
     emphasizing operations, fire direction, and limited target acquisition and intelligence functions. The main CP
     would follow as soon as possible and monitor the situation, prepared to assume control, as required. The
     main CP continues to conduct planning and other CP functions to allow the tactical CP to concentrate on the
     current fight during a critical stage in the battle.

     2-70. The tactical CP will consist of only one or two vehicles that have the speed and maneuverability to
     keep up with the maneuver forces. The tactical CP should have a robust communications capability as it may
     operate separate from the main CP for a longer period and must be fully capable of controlling the battalion's
     fires and directing subordinate elements. The tactical CP technique is also useful when the commander wants
     to send a CP element forward with an artillery raid.

ADDITIONAL COMMAND POST MOVEMENT TECHNIQUES

     2-71. Security, organization for combat, personnel strength, equipment status, availability of mutually
     supporting battalions, and the tactical situation influences movement of the CP. The CP may move as a single
     unit, or in several small elements. A major consideration in determining the CP movement technique is the
     availability of an element to assume the CP functions for continuity of operations. For example, a primary
     computer system at the FA battalion could conduct continuity of operations with the brigade FSE automated
     system, or a firing platoon FDC primary computer system. Rehearsals and clear tactical SOPs prevent
     confusion.

LIAISON OPERATIONS

     2-72. One of the inherent responsibilities of a FA battalion assigned a R or a GSR is to provide liaison to the
     reinforced unit. The battalion commander may direct liaison to be established with another FA unit, a
     maneuver unit, or any other element. Liaison may be necessary with nonmilitary elements. FA battalions,
     establish liaison teams from existing resources.

     2-73. Liaison is the contact or intercommunication maintained between elements to ensure mutual
     understanding and unity of purpose and action. Liaison activities augment the commander's ability to
     synchronize and focus combat power. Liaison includes establishing and maintaining physical contact and
     communications. Establish liaison activities to ensure cooperation and understanding between commanders
     and staffs; coordination on tactical matters to achieve mutual purpose, support, and action; exact and precise
     understanding of implied or inferred coordination measures to achieve synchronized results.

     2-74. Overall, liaison becomes another tool to help commanders gain assurance that supporting and supported
     commanders understand implicit coordination and achieve synchronized results. Effective liaison enhances
     the commander's confidence in planning and in mission execution.

     2-75. If the reinforced and R units have compatible communications and automation equipment, and both
     units maintain communications and situational understanding, the actual physical presence of a liaison team
     at the reinforced unit may not be required. If the units choose to collocate CPs or FDCs, no additional liaison
     is required.

     2-76. When an FA battalion is assigned a support relationship of GS, it will normally be positioned in the
     assigned area of the supported unit, usually a division. The GS FA battalion commander may consider
     sending a liaison team to the division FSE. This team can help the battalion commander track the maneuver
     situation and keep the DIVARTY commander informed of the location and status of the FA battalion that is
     in the area but not under the DIVARTYs control.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  33


---

Chapter 2

     2-77. Additionally, regionally aligned security force assistance brigades have fires advisor teams in their FA
     battalion embedded with host/partner nation at echelon who maintain U.S based communication systems to
     enable communication between partner and U.S. forces. Part of their advisor mission is to serve as liaisons
     and facilitate alliance based military operations. They are a valuable resource that U.S. forces can access to
     help coordinate their operations with an adjacent partner force who will save that force from needing to
     allocate additional personnel or resources.

     2-78. SFAB FA advisor teams ensure allies/partners are involved in FS and FA tactical/technical rehearsals,
     when possible, to ensure mutual understanding of US FA units’ plans, locations, movements, and ensures
     U.S. forces are aware of the supported forces FS and FA tactical plans.

SECTION III – FIELD ARTILLERY BATTALION COMMUNICATIONS

     2-79. The commander must develop techniques that promote an expeditious flow of information throughout
     the unit. The techniques developed are listed in the unit's SOP. Units' base decisions on the information
     derived from the C2 processes and activities:

          • Acquire information.
          • Assess whether new actions are required.
          • Determine what these actions should be.
          • Direct subordinates to take appropriate actions.
          • Supervise and assess.

     2-80. C2 includes the facilities, personnel, networks, information systems, processes, and procedures
     essential to a commander to enable C2. The C2 system of the FA battalion is the CP, the personnel that
     operate the CP, and a mixture of automation systems and the communications systems with which they
     operate.

AUTOMATED SYSTEMS

     2-81. Automated systems provide the electromagnetic architecture in which situational understanding is
     accomplished. Units are equipped with automated systems, providing them significant advantages in
     collecting technical information, and distributing information and intelligence rapidly. The automated
     systems enable the commander to rapidly gain reliable information and achieve information dominance in
     their operational environment. The automated systems satisfy two critical requirements: interoperability, and
     situational understanding. These systems pass critical information at decisive times, which leverage and
     exploit tactical success and facilitate future operations.

     2-82. At the FA battalion, assigned operators use internet protocol services to establish data and voice
     communications to the HQ. Setup for the network is a standard procedure that consists of powering the
     system, pointing to the satellite, and connecting the data equipment for needed dial tone or e-mail and
     organizational traffic.

     2-83. The battalion vertically and horizontally integrates automated systems at the tactical and operational
     levels. The digital message capabilities of the automated systems provide the FA battalion commander a
     variety of tools to assist in command of the battalion.

COMMUNICATION NETWORKS

     2-84. The FA battalions rely on a federation of communication networks that collectively enable C2. Not all
     components of the network are under their control. The network is a critical tool in operations and must be
     redundant, flexible, and adaptive to the commander. It is important that leaders are familiar with the
     capabilities and limitations of the architecture and components of the current communications network.

     2-85. For more information on the unit’s various communication networks see appropriate technical manuals
     and unit SOPs.

34  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                                    Field Artillery Battalion Command and Control

FIELD ARTILLERY BATTALION RADIO COMMUNICATION
OPERATIONS

     2-86. Radio communications are critical to the operation of the FA battalion. The different responsibilities
     and areas that are essential to radio communications success are as follows.

RADIO COMMUNICATIONS RESPONSIBILITIES

     2-87. Radio communication is a command responsibility and essential to efficient command of the FA
     battalion and enhances its ability to provide effective fires. The commander's radio communications plan will
     include supported, reinforced, and adjacent units. To establish a responsive and dependable communications
     system, the FA battalion must consider and work within the following parameters, to include:

          • Rely heavily on frequency modulation radio communications.
          • Monitor multiple radio networks while maintaining continuity of operations during displacement.
          • Communicate over long distances to many diverse elements, such as FIST, FSEs, R units,

                DIVARTY, FAB, and the supported force.
          • Rely increasingly on digital data communications, which have shorter-range capabilities than voice

                communications.

     2-88. The FA battalion S-6 has overall authority and direction of the communications assets and operations
     within the FA battalion. The battalion S-6 must work closely with the brigade S-6 to ensure efficient radio
     communications for FS throughout the supported higher HQ AO. The S-6 maintains the communications and
     automation systems, in addition operates retransmission stations for the FA battalion. The S-6 has a section
     HQ, an automation management section, and a retransmission section.

     2-89. Elements of the battalion send communications equipment for repair through the S-6. The
     communications maintenance personnel perform maintenance on battalion HQ communications equipment
     within their capability. Repair personnel in the FSC provide on-site field maintenance and assistance for the
     subordinate units of the battalion.

     2-90. The automation management section assists with the establishment, operation, and maintenance of the
     battalion's automation systems. This includes hardware, software, networks, and automation security. The
     section's networking functions include management and maintenance internal to the FA battalion local area
     network.

     2-91. The retransmission section establishes and maintains the retransmission station, as required. Dual
     retransmission capability is essential to maintaining digital and voice communications over extended
     distances or due to areas of rough and complex terrain.

ECHELONS OF COMMAND

     2-92. The senior unit is responsible for establishing communications links with its subordinate units. This
     responsibility is primarily one of planning and directing the establishment of the communications links since
     assets belonging to either the senior HQ or the subordinate unit may be used. The supported higher HQ has
     the responsibility to establish communications with the FA battalion and the FA battalion HQ has
     responsibility to establish communications with its organic elements. The battalion's responsibilities for
     establishing communications include:

          • Senior to subordinate. A senior unit is responsible for establishing communications with a
                subordinate unit. An attached unit of any size is subordinate to the attached command.

          • Supporting to supported. A supporting unit is responsible for establishing communications with the
                supported unit.

          • R to Reinforced. A reinforcing unit is responsible for establishing communications with the
                reinforced unit.

          • Passing to stationary. In a forward passage of lines, the passing unit is responsible for establishing
                communications with the stationary unit.

          • Stationary to passing. In a rearward passage of lines, the stationary unit is responsible for
                establishing communications with the passing unit.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  35


---

Chapter 2

          • Lateral communications. The next higher commander or SOP directs responsibility for establishing
                communications between adjacent units. If responsibility is not directed by orders, the commander
                of the unit on the left is responsible for establishing communications with the unit on the right. The
                commander of a unit positioned behind another unit establishes communications with the forward
                unit.

          • Restoration. Regardless of the responsibility, all units take prompt action to restore lost
                communications.

AREA OF OPERATIONS

     2-93. Adjacent commands must maintain communications with each other to ensure coordination of the
     combat effort. When facing the FLOT (left-to-right relationship), the command on the left establishes
     communications with the command on its right. In a noncontiguous AO, units will establish and maintain
     communications with adjacent units that can mutually support or influence their operations.

RESTORING AND REESTABLISHING COMMUNICATIONS

     2-94. Regardless of which unit is responsible for establishing communications, all units served by the system
     must help restore any communications system outage. This means that units will work from higher to lower
     and lower to higher to reestablish communications when interrupted and not wait for the network control
     station to solve the problem.

PLANNING CONSIDERATION

     2-95. The communications plan supports the command requirements of a support relationship. To meet
     specific requirements commanders may modify their systems based on METT-TC (I). The S-6 assists the S-
     3 in developing the communications plan during the MDMP. There are factors to be considered during the
     planning phase, to include:

          • Digital and voice networks. FA battalions use a mix of digital and voice radio networks as
                determined by the operational status of assigned equipment and mission variables. If digital
                capability is lost by the battalion or by one or more of the batteries, the voice networks can quickly
                become overburdened. The FA battalion communications plan must include plans for converting
                some digital networks to voice while continuing to support the remaining digital data networks. The
                communication plan should include a plan for reconverting to digital networks. It is extremely
                important to keep voice traffic off digital networks and vice versa. Develop and rehearse frequently
                a voice backup plan for each operation. Many of the details can be included in the unit tactical SOP.

          • Planning ranges. Range capabilities vary with the method of communications, the type and model
                of equipment used, terrain, weather, and atmospheric conditions. Jamming also degrades
                communications range. Since rehearsal on the actual terrain and under the exact conditions is often
                impossible, experience, digital terrain tools, and thorough map reconnaissance are essential to
                proper estimation of communications ranges.

          • System mixes. The factors of METT-TC (I) have different effects on the communications means.
                The preferred communications setup is any system or mixture of systems that will communicate the
                information with the least exposure to enemy electromagnetic warfare and not place total reliance
                on radio. A good communications plan maximizes the use of all available systems and backup plans
                to prevent over reliance on any one system. Address much of a FA battalion's basic communications
                planning in the unit's tactical SOP.

COMMUNICATIONS SECURITY CONSIDERATIONS

     2-96. COMSEC should be part of each FA battalion tactical SOP. Effective COMSEC techniques include:
          • Require authentication on non-secure networks if operating in the single channel mode of operation.

             Note. proper authentication procedures can eliminate intrusion and imitative deception.

          • Never mix plain and encrypted traffic on the same network. Doing so compromises the nature of
                the network, which makes interception and analysis easier for the enemy.

36  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                               Field Artillery Battalion Command and Control

          • Use secure equipment whenever possible. If the battalion is supporting a unit without secure
                capability, specify networks that will be unsecured and enforce secure discipline on all remaining
                networks.

          • Limit transmissions to five seconds or less, if operating in the single channel mode of operation.
                This makes interception and direction finding more difficult.

          • Work through jamming if possible. Report suspected jamming on a different network to supported
                higher HQ or the network control station immediately. Do not stop using the suspected network as
                this may tip the enemy that their jamming attempts were successful and friendly forces will not be
                able to pinpoint the jamming location if they stop and start jamming a new channel or frequency.
                Jumping networks should be a last resort. Remember that if jamming is bad enough to keep a unit
                from operating on a network, it may also keep many of the stations from receiving the signal to
                change frequencies. Disseminate anti-jam frequencies well in advance, so that subscriber stations
                can move to the alternate frequencies in sequence. Reduce susceptibility to jamming by using
                frequency-hopping radios.

          • Schedule and use only authorized call signs from the automated network control device or SOI.

INTERNAL AND EXTERNAL COMMUNICATIONS NETWORKS

     2-97. The FA battalion communicates through internal and external communication networks. The details on
     both internal and external networks follows.

Internal Networks

     2-98. The FA battalion will require multiple internal networks for normal operations. The various networks
     that may be required for normal battalion operations include:

          • Use battalion command (voice) network for command, collection, and dissemination of tactical
                information and intelligence. The battalion operations section is the network control station.

          • Use battalion fire direction (digital) networks for tactical and technical fire direction from the
                forward observer through the FA battalion FDC to the controlling platoon FDC. Assign the fire
                direction networks according to the mission and the battalion's communications status, not
                necessarily one per platoon. However, a technique frequently used under ideal communications, is
                to assign a network per firing platoon, with the forward observers, FIST, maneuver battalion FSE,
                and battalion FDC spread evenly across the networks. In some operational situations, when platoons
                are firing units, it will be required to allocate a fire direction network per firing platoon. The network
                control station for all fire direction networks is the battalion FDC. Establish one or more of the
                networks as a voice network.

          • Use battalion command (digital) networks for FS planning and coordination between FA elements,
                for mutual support unit operations, and for tactical and technical fire direction to R artillery units.
                The network control station is the battalion FDC. Establish the network as a voice network during
                degraded operations.

          • Battalion operations and intelligence (digital) network are used for exchanging targeting, counterfire
                section, survey, meteorological data, information and intelligence within the battalion. These
                elements can include radars, target processing section, and survey teams. The network control
                station is the battalion S-3 section.

          • Battalion administration and logistics (voice) network is used for coordinating battalion
                administrative and logistical matters. The battalion trains CP is the network control station. The FA
                battalion S-4, S-1, the FSC commander, and subordinate platoon leaders will operate in this network
                on a full-time basis. The firing battery commanders, 1SGs, or XO will operate in this network, as
                required.

External Network

     2-99. The FA battalion may operate on several external networks. The actual number will depend on
     availability of communications assets, supported higher HQ task organization, and the division or corps
     organization with supporting brigades. The FA battalion may operate numerous external networks, to include
     as required:

          • BCT (voice).

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  37


---

Chapter 2

          • BCT operations and intelligence (voice).
          • BCT administrative and support (voice).
          • FAB network (digital) as assigned.
          • FAB operations and intelligence (digital), as assigned.
          • DIVARTY network (digital) as assigned.
          • DIVARTY operations and intelligence (digital), as assigned.
          • BCT FS network (voice).
          • Maneuver battalion FS network (voice).
          • BCT FSE and battalion or squadron FSEs may also operate on a naval gunfire (voice) network.

COMMUNICATIONS TECHNIQUES

     2-100. Communications operations must take advantage of all techniques to facilitate mission
     accomplishment. Communication operational techniques should be considered and employed to enhance the
     FA battalion command capability, which may include:

          • Remote Transmitters. Using remote transmitters separates the radio frequency emitter from the CP
                or from other critical facilities. In addition, remote radios allow transmitters to be sited for optimum
                communications while allowing the user to position in locations better suited to survivability.
                Remote transmitters also minimize on-site or mutual interference while dissipating and reducing
                electromagnetic signature.

          • Retransmission. The FA battalion should use retransmission operations to extend the area of
                coverage of a specific radio network or to reduce the electromagnetic signature of a position. Reduce
                radio frequency power output using a retransmission site at the CP or other location. Double overall,
                network ranges by the effective use of retransmission. Terrain and obstacles restrict the transmitting
                distances of frequency modulation and very high frequency transmissions. The positioning of radio
                equipment is often critical. Techniques for using frequency modulation retransmission include:
                 Conduct a map reconnaissance of the AO. The S-6, S-2, and S-3 should discuss retransmission
                     operations during the planning phase.
                 Analyze the terrain for optimum communications; use the digital terrain tools to assist in this
                     analysis.
                 Select primary and alternate locations for retransmission. Consider accessibility, security, and
                     logistical support.
                 Arrange the timetable for site occupation and network operation.
                 Train operators on retransmission procedures.
                 Ensure users are aware of the tactical situation and how retransmission works.
                 Program additional key and delay time to allow radios to key up before transmitting.
                 Plan to use a nearby firing platoon, battery, FIST, or supported higher HQ FSE to relay
                     messages. This capability is useful when direct communication with an element is not possible.
                     Relay addressing should be established per tactical SOP or as identified in the SOI.

          • Antenna Multiplexers. Using antenna multiplexers reduces the number of ground plane antennas
                required to operate multiple radios. The time required to align and tune these devices is considerably
                less than the time required to install multiple antennas. However, multiplexers also have several
                disadvantages, to include reduced communications range and significantly increased bit error rate
                for digital traffic. Note: the rate will vary for different frequencies.

          • Directional Antennas. Directional antennas reduce electromagnetic signature in two directions while
                extending the range of the radio along the long axis of the antenna. Use directional antennas in
                single-channel mode, not frequency-hopping mode.

     2-101. A robust digital and voice communications network that can transmit over an extended range is
     essential for successful operations. Interrupted communications include obstructions to line of sight, other
     systems operating in the same electromagnetic spectrum, atmospheric conditions, and enemy electromagnetic
     attack. Planning communications is increasingly complex and requires participation by every staff member,
     not just the S-6. Planning should include at a minimum:

          • Include a detailed communications line of sight analysis (digital terrain tools or a communication
                modified combined obstacle overlay [MCOO]).

          • Provide for alternate means of communications.

38  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                                    Field Artillery Battalion Command and Control

          • Ensure subordinates understand the commander's intent so that they know what to do if
                communications are lost.

          • Plan communications architectures in detail, including the use of retransmission, digital network
                linkages, and CP placement.

          • Develop and distribute an overlay that depicts retransmission, digital network links, and command
                nodes in the brigade's AO. This communication overlay enables communications node and terrain
                management deconfliction, prevents fratricide, and provides for an increase in communication
                efficiency by reducing communications signature, profile, and request for information.

          • Define the initial task organization and expected changes to establish and maintain the tactical
                internet.

          • Follow proper signal and COMSEC procedures.
          • Keep digital orders, overlays, and messages concise to avoid overloading the tactical internet and

                digital systems.
          • Establish SOP for when and what communications will be transmitted digitally, and which will be

                transmitted by voice.
          • Establish alternate communications procedures.

Commercial Lines and Cell Phones

     2-102. Use commercial lines when approved by supported higher HQ. Consider securing commercial lines
     using devices like the secure telephone unit and limiting classified material sent over non-secure lines and
     on cell phones. Threat forces may rely heavily on local communications networks.

Sound and Visual

     2-103. Sound and visual signals include pyrotechnics, hand-and-arm, flags, metal-on-metal, and sirens.
     Ensure the instructions for the use of signals are in the SOI or the unit SOP. Establish signals not included in
     the SOI in the SOP or in the OPORD. The battlefield will have many sound and visual cues that become
     increasingly important in complex and urban terrain. Commanders and staff planners must carefully
     determine how sound and visual signals will be used and authenticated.

Messengers and Liaison Officers

     2-104. Use messengers between the CPs of higher and lower HQ in the event of communications failures or
     enemy jamming. Send liaisons to supported higher HQ to speed the passage of information between CPs.
     The use of LNOs becomes increasingly important as digitally equipped units operate with non-digitally
     equipped units. The LNOs provide access to the digital COP.

Frequency Modulation (voice) Communications

     2-105. The FA battalion should not rely on digital communications alone. Digitization does not eliminate the
     requirement for maps and voice communications. Digitized systems facilitate collaborative planning and
     rapid dissemination of plans and orders. The FA battalion commander and staff must learn to use the
     automation systems extensively during the planning and preparation phase of any operation.

     2-106. Whether to use voice or digital means for communication is a function of the situation and SOPs.
     Even though both systems are critical for effective command at all levels, voice may be the primary method
     for control.

     2-107. Voice radio remains the primary means of communications after crossing the line of departure because
     it is more responsive, multiple stations can monitor the network, and parties convey emotion during the
     transmission, which is a critical tool in assessing and understanding the battlefield situation.

     2-108. Voice is the primary method of communications when supported elements are in contact and time is
     a critical factor. The FA battalion commander and staff must remain sensitive to the difficulty and danger of
     using digital systems when units are moving or in contact and not expect digital reports under those
     conditions. The FA battalion commander and staff must continue to update digital databases and COP. Use
     digital systems for reporting combat information when not in direct fire contact. Company and troop CPs

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  39


---

Chapter 2

     serve as the critical link in turning the voice reports received by units in contact into the digital reports that
     generate the COP. Other general guidelines may include:

          • Report initial contact at any echelon within the brigade on voice, as this alerts the network that a
                digital threat spot report will follow as soon as possible.

          • Use voice for elements on the move (not in CPs), unless they can stop and generate a digital message
                or report.

          • Initiate emergency logistical requests, especially CASEVAC requests, on voice with a follow-up
                digital report if possible.

          • Transmit threat spot reports for reconnaissance elements moving or in contact on voice; their
                supported higher HQ should convert voice reports into digital spot reports to generate situational
                understanding.

          • Submit calls for fire on targets of opportunity digitally or by voice.
          • Submit digital planned calls for fire from FIST in the initial part of an engagement.
          • Submit digital routine logistics reports and requests.
          • Submit digital routine reports prior to and following combat.
          • Submit digital orders, plans, and graphics, accompanied by a voice call to alert recipients that they

                have critical information. Additionally, the transmitting element should request a verbal
                acknowledgement of both receipt and understanding of the transmitted information by an
                appropriate Soldier.
          • Submit voice obstacle and CBRN reports followed by digital reports to generate a geo-referenced
                situational understanding message portraying the obstacle or contaminated area across the network.

40  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026
