     rounds per tube per day.

7-49. Ammunition supplies are provided from the supported higher HQ ammunition transfer holding point
(within the brigade support area) to the FSC. They are carried forward with the next logistics package, or
immediately, as required. Ammunition and explosives are accounted for and provide proper physical security.

CLASS VI

7-50. Soldiers usually carry 30 days of personal comfort items with them when deploying. They replenish
their own supplies through the Army and Air Force Exchange System or local purchase.

7-51. Health and comfort packs provide forward area troops everyday necessities required when the exchange
system is not available. Delivery of health and comfort packs is based on headcounts provided for field
feeding.

CLASS VII

7-52. Units normally deploy with required equipment based off their modified table of organization and
equipment. There may be additional equipment issued in the joint operations area. Battery commanders must
ensure their supply SGTs establish accountability on new equipment by creating hand receipts from packing
lists or inventory. Report new equipment through the battalion S-4 and brigade S-4 to the appropriate property

25 February 2026                       ATP 3-09.23                                                                99


---

Chapter 7

     book officer. Property book officers account for and order Class VII and other non-expendable items in the
     supported higher HQ.

     7-53. Class VII replacement is based on losses reported through command channels to the brigade S-3 and
     S-4 per unit SOP. This permits the commander to remain apprised of the operational status of subordinate
     commands, and to direct the distribution of items to those units having the most critical need. Replacement
     Class VII equipment is delivered to the supported higher HQ in the brigade support area. The brigade S-4
     and BSB sustainment officer should confirm if replacement weapons systems (for example, howitzers) will
     be delivered with basic issue items, additional authorized list, munitions, and crew. This allows the battalion
     S-3 and S-4 to better apportion replacements. Low density, specialized equipment in the battalion may require
     additional evaluation by Department of the Army civilians or contractors before classifying it as a loss.

CLASS VIII

     7-54. Role 1 medical treatment facilities deploy with a three-day supply of consumable medical supplies, and
     the battery deploys with fully stocked CLS bags. Individual Soldiers should deploy with a 180-day supply of
     their prescribed medications. As they deploy, Soldiers must advise their supporting medical personnel of
     their specific needs so the medical sustainment system can sustain these prescriptions.

CLASS IX

     7-55. Each battery stocks and deploys with combat spares for repair parts (for example, weapons and radio
     maintenance). Battery spares are a combination of prescribed load list, shop stock, and bench stock. The FSC
     also has combat spares or shop stock to support maintenance of vehicles, generators, and other equipment.

     7-56. The battery supply SGT and maintenance personnel replenish their combat spares and order other parts
     as needed through the FSC to the BSB. Class IX repair part requisitions are prioritized based on the
     commander's priority of maintenance and need.

CLASS X

     7-57. The FA battalion supports stability tasks operations by providing FS to assist in maintaining stability
     and security. This can include establishing secure operational areas to safely transport class X relief supplies
     for foreign humanitarian assistance and reconstruction efforts. For more information, you can refer to FM 3-
     07.

TRANSPORTATION

     7-58. The FA battalion is 100% mobile with organic vehicles and trailers. The BSB, including the FSC is
     mobile for organic equipment, and has been designed to be mobile with three combat loads for FA units. The
     BSB commander, in coordination with the supported commander, may choose to sacrifice mobility for extra
     supply stocks. If the BSB's limited assets are committed, and extra transportation is required, the brigade S-
     4 requests transportation assets from higher HQ.

SECTION V – EXPLOSIVE ORDNANCE DISPOSAL

     7-59. Explosive ordnance disposal (EOD) capabilities are not organic to FA units. Request EOD
     augmentation from higher HQ to support operations. Requests for EOD support are processed through the
     brigade or division, who then forwards the request to the supporting EOD HQ.

     7-60. Once unexploded ordnance is located, the locating unit will send an EOD 9 Line Report through their
     chain of command requesting EOD support. If there is a constant presence of explosive hazards, such as
     explosive ordnance, EOD teams may be assigned a DS or GS support relationship to the FA battalion.

100  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                               Field Artillery Battalion Sustainment

SECTION VI – FINANCIAL MANAGEMENT

     7-61. The battalion has no organic financial management support assets. Financial management detachments
     are deployed to provide area financial support to a brigade or equivalent size unit, or as directed by the
     financial management company commander.

     7-62. Financial management support includes banking and currency support, disbursing support, tracking
     cost and accounting, host nation support, and limited pay support. Typical finance requirements of the FA
     battalion include temporary duty requests, purchase requests and commitments, and multinational support.

SECTION VII – HUMAN RESOURCES SUPPORT

     7-63. Human resources support operations are embedded within the battalion S-1 section. The S-1 plans,
     provides, and coordinates the delivery of human resources support, services, or information to all assigned
     or attached personnel.

     7-64. Human resources support beyond the capability of the S-1 is coordinated with the BSB S-1. The S-1 is
     the coordinating office for medical, financial management, and the Red Cross. For more information, see
     ATP 1-0.1.

SECTION VIII – RELIGIOUS SUPPORT

     7-65. Each battalion has a unit ministry team, consisting of a chaplain and religious affairs assistant, to
     provide religious support to their Soldiers. The chaplain serves the commander as a personal staff officer.

     7-66. The chaplain plans, synchronizes, and coordinates religious support, within the battalion AO. The
     DIVARTY unit ministry team is responsible for the technical oversight of the unit ministry teams in
     subordinate units. For more information see ATP 1-05.01.

SECTION IX – LEGAL SUPPORT

     7-67. The brigade legal section provides legal support to the command, brigade staff, and subordinate
     battalions across the Judge Advocate General's Corps legal disciplines: military justice, international and
     operational law, administrative and civil law, contract and fiscal law, claims, and legal assistance. The Judge
     Advocate usually locates the legal section in the main CP to provide immediate support to the commander
     and the fires element.

     7-68. Each battalion typically has a paralegal NCO assigned to the S-1 section to provide immediate legal
     support to battalion commanders and staff, although they remain under the supervisory control of the judge
     advocate. See FM 3-84, Legal Support to Operations, for additional information about the legal section
     support.

SECTION X – ARMY HEALTH SYSTEM

MEDICAL PLATOON

     7-69. The medical platoon is the focal point of Army health system support for the battalion. The platoon
     receives, treats, and evacuate patients and coordinates further MEDEVAC, as required. It establishes and
     operates the BAS, which is the primary role 1 medical treatment facility for the battalion. For more
     information on medical platoon operations, refer to ATP 4-02.4.

COMBAT LIFESAVERS

     7-70. The CLS is a nonmedical Soldier selected by the unit commander for additional training beyond basic
     first aid procedures. A minimum of one individual per squad, crew, team, or equivalent-size unit should be
     trained. The primary duty of this individual does not change. The additional duty of the CLS is to provide

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  101


---

Chapter 7

     enhanced first aid for injuries before the combat medic arrives. The CLS is usually the first person on the
     scene to begin the process of providing enhanced first aid to wounded and injured personnel. The CLS is not
     intended to take the place of medical personnel, but to stabilize a wounded or injured Soldier's condition until
     medical personnel arrive.

SECTION XI – MAINTENANCE

     7-71. The Army has two levels of maintenance, field and sustainment. Field maintenance consists primarily
     of replacing parts on the user's system. It is generally a merging of organizational and DS levels of
     maintenance. Field-level maintainers are concentrated in the BSB, either in the supported higher HQ BSB's
     FSC or the field maintenance company.

     7-72. Sustainment maintenance consists of repairing components removed from the user's system.
     Sustainment maintenance is generally a merging of the former GS and depot levels of maintenance.
     Generally, sustainment-level maintainers are in the sustainment brigade, usually in a component repair
     company.

FIELD MAINTENANCE

     7-73. Field maintenance is on-system maintenance and is mainly preventive maintenance and replacement
     of defective parts. Field maintenance returns repaired equipment to the Soldier. It includes some off-system
     maintenance critical to mission readiness.

     7-74. Battery commanders ensure that vehicle crews and equipment operators perform preventative
     maintenance checks and services. To provide quick turnaround of maintenance problems, each battery has a
     field maintenance team from their supporting FSC dedicated to support them. These field maintenance teams
     have contact maintenance trucks and mechanics trained in the company's equipment.

     7-75. The FSC performs field maintenance. The FSC has a maintenance platoon that repairs automotive,
     armament, ground support, electronic, and missile equipment. The FSC focuses online replaceable unit
     replacement, using combat repair team and field maintenance team stocks, on board spares and shop stock.
     It has a service and recovery section and performs battle damage assessment and repair. The FSC's
     maintenance control section orders and manages repair parts. The FSC commander establishes unit
     maintenance collection points in coordination with the FA battalion S-4.

     7-76. The maintenance platoon leader task-organizes the maintenance platoon according to directives
     received from the FSC commander. The battalion XO, S-3, S-4, and FSC commander analyze the current
     and anticipated support requirements and recommend to the FA battalion commander the maintenance
     concept to be used. They will recommend to the commander the appropriate support at battery, maintenance
     collection point, CP, and trains locations.

     7-77. In most tactical situations, the FSC will provide each FA battery a maintenance support team. In some
     instances, the FSC's recovery vehicles may also be positioned forward with each firing battery but remain
     under battalion control. This provides a quick-fix capability for those items that can be repaired quickly
     without hindering tactical operations and a recovery capability for those items requiring repairs that are more
     extensive.

     7-78. The rest of the maintenance platoon is in the combat trains under the control of the maintenance platoon
     SGT. The platoon maintenance section performs maintenance on FSC and HHB equipment and assists the
     field maintenance teams, as required. Maintenance of low density, specialized equipment usually requires
     maintenance by Department of the Army civilians or contractors. The battalion S-4 must coordinate through
     the FSC for specific management procedures for this maintenance.

102  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                                                   Field Artillery Battalion Sustainment

SUSTAINMENT MAINTENANCE

     7-79. Sustainment maintenance is off-system component repair and/or end item repair and return to the
     supply system or by exception to the owning unit, performed by national level maintenance providers (ATP
     4-33). Sustainment maintenance actions typically involve repair of repairable Class IX components, off-
     system, for return to the supply system. Examples of sustainment maintenance include inside-the-box repair
     of line replaceable units and rebuild of engines and transmissions.

     7-80. A repairable is an item that can be cost-effectively repaired. When a repairable such as a diesel engine
     or turbine fuel control malfunctions, it can be replaced by a repaired or rebuilt component; it usually does
     not need to be replaced by a new item. Although the mechanics in the supported higher HQ cannot repair
     unserviceable repairables, the component repair companies in the sustainment brigade need those
     unserviceables to create serviceable repair parts.

SECTION XII – SUPPORT AREAS

     7-81. A support area is the portion of the commander’s area of operations that is designated to facilitate the
     positioning, employment, and protection of base sustainment assets required to sustain, enable, and control
     operations (ADP 3-0). Types of support areas include the brigade support area, battalion trains, and battery
     trains.

     7-82. The brigade support area is the logistical, personnel, and administrative hub of the supported higher
     HQ. It consists of the BSB but could also include a supported higher HQ alternate CP, battalion field trains,
     brigade engineer battalion units, air and missile defense assets, signal assets, and other sustainment units
     from higher HQ. The brigade support area is located to maintain support to the supported higher HQ, but not
     to interfere with the tactical movement of supported higher HQ units, or with units that must pass through
     the supported higher HQ area. Usually, the brigade support area is on a main supply route and ideally is out
     of the range of the enemy's medium artillery. The brigade support area should be positioned away from the
     enemy's likely avenues of approach.

TRAINS

     7-83. Trains are a unit grouping of personnel, vehicles, and equipment to provide sustainment. It is the basic
     sustainment tactical organization. Battalions use trains to array their subordinate sustainment elements,
     including their FSC. Battalion trains usually are under the control of the battalion S-4 and assisted by the
     battalion S-1.

     7-84. The composition and location of battalion trains varies depending on the number of units attached to,
     or augmenting, the battalion. The battalion trains can be employed in two basic configurations, as unit trains
     or as echeloned trains. See ATP 3-90.5 for additional information on sustainment trains.

UNIT TRAINS

     7-85. When logistical support resources are centralized in one location, they are called unit trains. There are
     benefits to utilizing this option, which include:

          • Coordinated control and a single base for logistical personnel and equipment.
          • Enhanced security and capability for ground defense.

     7-86. Unit trains may be appropriate in slow-moving or static situations, when the tactical situation forces
     the trains to be a self-contained operation, during reconstitution, when the battalion is in an assembly area,
     or during an extended tactical march. Unit trains normally consist of the FSC sustainment assets, except for
     field maintenance teams positioned with the firing batteries and possibly a maintenance collection point. The
     maintenance collection point may be positioned closer to the firing batteries or near the FA battalion CP to
     facilitate rapid repair and evacuation of equipment. The FSC commander is responsible for and commands
     unit trains.

     7-87. Towed FA battalions normally lack sufficient resources to effectively use the echeloned trains concept
     and typically operate under a unit trains organization. The unit trains are normally in or near the brigade

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  103


---

Chapter 7

     support area. The commander will configure the field artillery battalion trains to meet operational
     requirements. For an example of how field and combat trains are configured see Table 7-6.

                        Table 7-6. Field trains and combat trains (example)

Field Trains                          Combat Trains

FSC (-)                               FSC (-) FWD

FSC Command and First Sergeant        Field Maintenance Teams

Maintenance Platoon Headquarters (-)  Maintenance Collection Point

Maintenance Section                   Maintenance Control Section

General Supply Section                Class III Section (-)

Class III Section (-)                 Class V Section (-)

Class V Section (-)                   Field Artillery Battalion S-1 and S-4 (-)

Field Artillery Battalion S-1 and S-4 (-)

FSC – forward support company, FWD - forward, S-1 - battalion or brigade personnel staff officer, S-4 – battalion or
brigade logistics staff office

ECHELONED TRAINS

     7-88. The battalion trains may consist of three types (for example, battery trains, combat trains and field
     trains). The preferred method of supporting the SP FA battalion is through echeloned trains. There are also
     benefits to utilizing this option, which include:

          • Immediate responsive forward support tailored to the tactical situation.
          • Flexible resource usage.
          • Increased resource survivability and enhanced responsiveness when the tactical situation is fluid or

               when the battalion is operating over extended distances.

BATTERY TRAINS

     7-89. Battery trains may be formed to provide sustainment for a battery during combat operations. The battery
     organizes its organic and attached sustainment assets into its battery trains, which usually include the 1SG
     and medical aid and evacuation teams. The supply SGT and armorer are generally located in the battalion
     field trains. Generally, the battery trains include a maintenance support team from the FSC, with capabilities
     for maintenance, recovery, and limited combat spares.

COMBAT TRAINS

     7-90. The battalion combat trains usually consist of the FSC and the BAS and are organized to provide
     immediate critical sustainment support to the FA batteries. The combat trains are the hub of sustainment
     operations for the battalion. When the combat trains are collocated with the battalion CP, the HHB
     commander may be designated as the commander of the combat trains. When the combat trains are located
     away from the HHB CP area, the FA battalion commander designates who commands the combat trains. The
     battalion trains CP is located in the combat trains, and the S-4 may be responsible along with the FSC
     commander for supervising and managing this CP.

     7-91. The S-4 section along with FSC personnel may form the basis of a combat trains CP, which may be
     established to plan and coordinate sustainment for tactical operations. Although the combat trains CP may
     serve as an alternate main CP in maneuver battalions, the combat trains CP typically does not have the
     communications and tactical fire control capabilities to do so for a FA battalion. If the combat trains CP is
     required to take command of the battalion, the automated system capability for tactical fire direction is
     provided from the battalion CP. When established, the combat trains CP usually consists of the elements of
     the FSC, battalion S-1, and battalion S-4. Most of the time, the S-4 is the officer in charge of the combat
     trains CP. There are situations that may dictate the need for a combat trains CP, which include:

          • Fast-moving, fluid operations.
          • BSB forward element operations.
          • Reception, staging, onward movement, and integration operations.

     7-92. Determine the optimum balance of combat trains elements, to include:

104                                   ATP 3-09.23                                25 February 2026


---

                                                                                                   Field Artillery Battalion Sustainment

          • The S-1 and S-4 (minus) combat trains CP.
          • Maintenance control section.
          • Petroleum, oil, and lubricant (for example, emergency resupply to the batteries and platoons).
          • Ammunition (for example, emergency distribution to the firing platoons).
          • BAS.
          • Decontamination assets.
          • Elements of the communications platoon.
          • Maintenance collection point.
          • Maintenance support teams with recovery, if not forward with batteries.

     7-93. The combat trains are located close enough to be responsive to the forward batteries and platoons but,
     if possible, they should not be within range of enemy indirect fire. They generally occupy an area between
     the brigade support area or the field trains (if deployed separately) and about one to four kilometers behind
     the forward battery or platoon PAA. Combat trains may move often to stay in supporting distance of the
     firing units.

     7-94. The combat trains CP is the focal point of battalion sustainment operations. The CP concentrates on
     ammunition and POL resupply, priority equipment repair and salvage, and emergency medical care. The
     combat trains post must stay abreast of the tactical situation to include monitoring the battalion command
     network, identifying logistic support requirements, and receiving requests, reports, and requirements from
     subordinate elements.

     7-95. The combat trains CP uses automated systems to maintain tactical situational awareness. The S-4 also
     operates the battle command sustainment support system (referred to as BCS3). This system provides the
     capability to monitor and track requests for and the status of repair parts and supplies for the FA battalion. It
     provides the FA battalion with the capability to develop sustainment estimates for future operations as well
     as provides the commander with the status of sustainment operations supporting current operations.

     7-96. Logistic personnel analyze, consolidate, and forward battalion requirements to the battalion
     sustainment center (field trains). The battalion sustainment center coordinates with and directs elements of
     the FSC in the FA battalion support area to take actions to meet the requirements of the forward units.

     7-97. The FA battalion often establishes a maintenance collection point to provide forward maintenance
     support to the battalion. The FSC commander may designate either the maintenance platoon leader or platoon
     SGT as the commander of the maintenance collection point.

     7-98. Under the echeloned trains concept, the maintenance collection point may locate in the combat trains,
     especially when increased security is required. However, the unit may also establish the maintenance
     collection point outside of, but near the combat trains to facilitate mission accomplishment, as required.

FIELD TRAINS

     7-99. Field trains include those assets not located with the combat trains. The field trains can provide direct
     coordination between the battalion and the BSB. The field trains personnel assist the coordination and
     movement of support from the BSB to the battalion by ensuring that logistics packages are organized and
     configured per the unit's requests and that the logistics packages make it forward to the release points and
     back to the brigade support area.

     7-100. The field trains may collocate with the brigade support area, operate independently between the
     brigade support area and combat trains, or collocate with trains of another FA or maneuver battalion. The
     threat and the distances between the forward elements of the battalion and the brigade support area affect the
     field trains' location.

     7-101. If the battalion is operating within the support distance of the brigade support area, the battalion field
     trains may collocate with the BSB in the brigade support area. This facilitates support by the BSB, eases
     communications requirements, simplifies security requirements, and reduces the need for additional
     coordination with the supported higher HQ for terrain. When collocating with the BSB, the field trains fall
     under the OPCON of the BSB commander for movement, security, terrain management, and synchronization
     of sustainment activities. The positioning needs of the battalion must be coordinated with the BSB. If
     possible, the battalion field trains should be positioned near the exit points of the brigade support area in

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  105


---

Chapter 7

     order to move forward to better support the battalion. If the battalion is operating at extended ranges from
     the brigade support area, the field trains may be positioned between the BSB and the battalion to better
     facilitate support.

     7-102. The battalion field trains CP may act as the primary direct coordination element between the FA
     battalion and the brigade support area. When established, the field trains CP usually consists of the elements
     of the FSC, FA battalion S-1 and S-4, and selected HHB personnel (for example, HHB commander or XO,
     1SG, CBRN NCO, and supply SGT). The FA battalion commander may determine the officer or NCO in
     charge of the field trains CP. Situations that may dictate the need for a field trains CP include periods of
     supply or resupply of major end items or periods when sustainment elements of the squadron are no longer
     100% mobile. The field trains CP tracks the current battle; plans for sustainment of future operations;
     provides sustainment representation to the main CP for planning and integration; forecasts and coordinates
     future requirements; and coordinates the return to duty of Soldiers and repaired equipment.

     7-103. The designated trains commander is responsible for trains security. In trains areas, a perimeter defense
     is planned. Elements in the trains are assigned a specific sector to defend. Mutually supporting positions that
     dominate likely avenues of approach are selected for vehicles armed with crew-served weapons. Reaction
     forces and observation posts are established in accordance with the tactical SOP. To enhance security, an
     alarm or warning system (such as rocket, artillery, missile warning) is arranged. Sector sketches, fire plans,
     and obstacle plans should be prepared. The designated trains commander directs rehearsals to ensure that
     personnel know the parts they play in the defensive scheme.

SECURITY OF SUPPORT AREAS

     7-104. Sustainment elements must organize and prepare to defend themselves against ground or air attacks.
     The number of support areas established depends upon METT-TC (I). The more areas that are established,
     the more difficult they become to defend as personnel and defensive means are spread over multiple locations.
     The security of the trains at each echelon is the responsibility of the individual in charge of the trains. The
     best defense is to avoid detection. Determine considerations that enable trains security, which include:

          • Select trains sites that use available cover, concealment, and camouflage.
          • Use movement and positioning discipline, as well as noise and light discipline to prevent detection.
          • Establish a perimeter defense.
          • Establish observation posts and patrols.
          • Position weapons for self-defense.
          • Plan mutually supporting positions.
          • Prepare a fire plan and make sector sketches.
          • Identify sectors of fires.
          • Emplace or select target reference points to control fires, and for use of indirect fires.
          • Integrate available combat vehicles within the trains (for example, vehicles awaiting maintenance

                or personnel) into the plan, and adjust the plan when vehicles depart.
          • Conduct rehearsals.
          • Establish rest plans.
          • Identify an alarm or warning system that would enable rapid execution of the defense plan without

                further guidance; the alarm, warning system, and defense plan are usually included in the SOP.
          • Designate a reaction force. Ensure the force is equipped to perform its mission. The ready reaction

                force must be well rehearsed or briefed on ROE.
          • Unit assembly.
          • Friendly and threat force recognition.
          • Actions on contact.

SUPPLY ROUTES

     7-105. The FA battalion S-4, in coordination with the FSC commander and battalion S-3, select supply routes
     between support areas. Main supply routes are routes designated within the supported higher HQ AO upon
     which the bulk of sustainment traffic flows in support of operations. Select a main supply route based on the
     terrain, friendly disposition, enemy situation, and scheme of maneuver.

106  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                               Field Artillery Battalion Sustainment

7-106. Plan alternate supply routes if a main supply route is interdicted by the enemy or becomes too
congested. In the event of CBRN contamination, either the primary or alternate main supply route may be
designated as the "dirty main supply route" to handle contaminated traffic. Alternate supply routes should
meet the same criteria as the main supply route. Military police and engineer units if available may assist
with regulating traffic and maintain routes. Security of supply routes may require the commander to commit
non-sustainment resources. See ATP 4-01.45 for convoy operations for further information.

7-107.  Determining route considerations, include:
     •    Location and planned scheme of maneuver for subordinate units.
     •    Location and planned movements of other units moving through the battalion's AO.
     •    Route characteristics such as route classification, width, obstructions, steep slopes, sharp curves,
          and type roadway surface.
     •    Two-way, all-weather traffic ability.
     •    Classification of bridges and culverts.
     •    Requirements for traffic control such as at choke points, congested areas, confusing intersections,
          or along built-up areas.
     •    Number and locations of crossover routes from the main supply route to alternate supply routes.
     •    Requirements for repair, upgrade, or maintenance of the route, fording sites, and bridges.
     •    Route vulnerabilities that must be protected. This may include bridges, fords, built-up areas, and
          choke points.
     •    Enemy threats such as air attack, conventional and unconventional tactics, mines, ambushes, and
          CBRN hazards.
     •    Known or likely locations of enemy penetrations, attacks, CBRN hazards, or obstacles.
     •    Known or potential civilian and dislocated civilian movements that must be controlled or monitored.

SECTION XIII – SUSTAINMENT SUPPORT PLANNING

     7-108. Sustainment planning must focus on tasks and systems that provide support and services to the
     battalion's Soldiers and weapon systems. Sustainment planning must address sustainment support during
     phases of an operation. The battalion staff develops the sustainment support plan concurrently with the
     tactical plan. Supporting sustainment plans must be as detailed as the tactical plan and fully synchronized
     and integrated. Use of tactical SOP and contingency plans greatly help the support staff officers in the
     planning effort. The FA battalion OPORD or the FASP addresses the key what, where, when, why, and how
     of logistic support issues as well as deviations from the tactical SOP.

     7-109. To develop and execute sound plans, logistic support personnel must achieve and maintain a high
     degree of situational awareness and initiate actions well before the start of operations they are to support.
     Careful management of the information flow demands that information requirements be clearly identified
     early in the process and shared with all involved.

     7-110. FA logistics preparation is a conscious effort to identify and assess factors that facilitate, inhibit, or
     deny support to the FA battalion. Logistic preparation involves a review of known EFATs involving
     sustainment support and the use of S-2 IPOE products as an aid in analyzing the operating, arming, fueling,
     fixing, moving, and sustaining factors. The goal is to determine the FA battalion logistic support requirements
     that will allow the development of a sustainment estimate and a feasible concept of support.

     7-111. The logistic preparation process requires that the FA battalion commander, XO, and S-3 understand
     data needed by sustainment support staff to plan and provide timely, effective support. It requires that the S-
     1, S-4, and the FSC commander understand the mission, the scheme of maneuver, fires, and battlefield time
     and space implications for support. Logistics preparation is a coordinated effort, which include:

          • Determine data requirements to support required actions.
          • Identify sources for pertinent data and collect raw or processed data.
          • Analyze collected data and develop it into decision information by assessing the impact on the

                mission and competing courses of action.
          • Integrate decision information into the MDMP by incorporating it into logistical estimates, fires

                plans, and actions.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  107


---

Chapter 7

     7-112. Determine sources that provide relevant logistical data, which include:
          • Supported higher HQ briefings, plans, and orders.
          • FA battalion commander's planning guidance and intent.
          • Operations and intelligence briefings and overlays.
          • Wargaming and rehearsals.
          • Organizational equipment, subordinate unit status reports, and route reconnaissance overlays.
          • Traffic circulation and highway regulation plans.

     7-113. A sustainment running estimate, which includes logistics preparation, is a continuous analysis of
     sustainment factors affecting mission accomplishment. Emphasis is on how the status of logistic support will
     affect proposed courses of action. Sustainment planners use this estimate to recommend the best COA that
     can be supported and to develop plans to support the operation.

     7-114. The sustainment running estimate at the FA battalion level is usually informal. At a minimum it is
     formulated in a briefing format that should address facts, assumptions, and conclusions, to include:

          • Human resources (for example, personnel strength data, gains and losses, status of key personnel,
                and casualty estimates).

          • Ordnance (for example, Class V status; restrictions, distribution system, RSR, CSR, expeditionary
                support package, maintenance, Class IX status, repair times, and evacuation policy).

          • Supply (for example, Class III [bulk] status, distribution system, and restrictions).
          • Transportation (for example, status of transportation assets, critical lines of communication, and

                main supply route status).
          • Sustainment of Soldiers and their systems (for example, Classes I, II, III, IV, VI, VII, IX, water, and

                field services status).
          • Courses of action that can be supported.

     7-115. Based on mission analysis, compare resources with requirements, evaluate shortfalls in close
     coordination with operations planners to determine their effect on selected courses of action.

     7-116. The sustainment staff must provide the sustainment running estimate in time for commanders to
     confirm the feasibility of the plan, modify plans and priorities as necessary, and calculate risks. Timely
     completion also facilitates the generation and coordination of supply and support requests.

SECTION XIV – REFUEL, REARM, RESUPPLY AND SURVEY POINT

     7-117. A battalion refuel, rearm, resupply and survey point site may be established to provide critical support
     to the battery. This technique involves the movement of critical battalion support elements (trains) to a
     location where the firing elements can pass through and take on needed ammunition and POL. Then the
     combat trains elements march-order and proceed from the site to a new location. The FA BN staff analyses
     the operational environment and provides options to the commander for the timing, location, methodology,
     and associated risk to force and mission in choosing to establish refuel, rearm, resupply and survey
     points. See figure 7-1 on page 110 for an example of a rearm, refuel, and resupply point in a permissive
     environment.

SELECTION

     7-118. The site should:
          • Be on or near the route of march for the firing elements.
          • Provide good trafficability.
          • Allow cover and concealment.

ORGANIZATION

     7-119. In organizing the site, the following should be considered.
          • Dispersion.
          • Camouflage.
          • Operations security.

108  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                                                   Field Artillery Battalion Sustainment

ACTIVITIES

     7-120. Some of the activities that can take place at the battalion refuel, rearm, resupply and survey point are
     as follows:

          • Key personnel (firing battery) briefing.
          • Tailoring to meet the needs of the unit.
          • POL, ammunition, maintenance, and rations (priorities) resupply.
          • Local security (augmented by firing battery).
          • Ammunition transfer point activities.

LOGISTICS RELEASE POINT

     7-121. The battery logistics release point (LRP) is established normally by the battery 1SG to push routine
     supplies forward and to facilitate other routine administration. Platoon SGTs meet the 1SG at the LRP daily
     or in accordance with the unit SOP.

                                                 Chapter 8

      Unique Characteristics of Individual Field Artillery
                                   Battalions

    Not all FA battalions are the same, each has unique characteristics based on the equipment
    assigned and the tasks that they must accomplish. In this chapter the differences from the
    generic FA battalion are listed for each unique FA battalion.

SECTION I – COMPOSITE FIELD ARTILLERY BATTALIONS

COMPOSITE TOWED FIELD ARTILLERY CANNON BATTALIONS

     8-1. A composite battalion is organized with different artillery weapon systems under one battalion HQ.
     Currently regular Army and ARNG composite battalions are structured the same. With an HHB, and three
     cannon batteries.

     8-2. The battalion has a HHB to provide C2 and administrative support. A FSC from a BSB provides
     logistical support. The battalion is organized with two batteries of towed 105mm howitzers (2x6) and one
     battery of 155mm towed howitzers (1x6). The battalion provides responsive and accurate fires to the Infantry
     BCTs or supported commander. See figure 8-1.

                                        Figure 8-1. Composite battalion

8-3. The HHB is organic to the battalion and is organized and equipped to coordinate administrative,
logistical, maintenance, and communications support for the battalion headquarters and firing batteries. The
functional elements of the HHB are shown in figure 8-2 on page 112.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  111


---

Chapter 8

                Figure 8-2. Composite battalion headquarters and headquarters battery

COMPOSITE FIELD ARTILLERY MISSILE BATTALIONS

     8-4. The long-range fires battalion (known as LRFB) plans, integrates, and directs command, control and
     administrative supervision for organic and attached units. The LRFB provides long-range and mid-range
     surface-to-surface theater strategic, and operational level fires in support of the joint force.

ORGANIZATION

     8-5. The battalion is organized with a HHB, three firing batteries, and an attached FSC. The HHB and FSC
     provide C2, administrative services, and logistical support for organic and attached elements. The battalion
     is tailorable and scalable to meet theater requirements. The attached FSC provides support to the battalion.
     See figure 8-3 on page 113.

                                                          Unique Characteristics of Individual Field Artillery Battalions

                                    Figure 8-3. Long-range fires battalion
8-6. The HHB operates as a tactical and administrative HQ. The HQ element will be positioned for the most
effective control of the battalion assets correlated with the terrain, combat mission, and responsiveness to the
higher HQ and supported units. The HHB provides command, control, administrative supervision, and
service support for organic and attached elements. It also procures, distributes, administers, and coordinates
supply transactions for all classes of supply. In addition, the HHB coordinates unit maintenance support not
found within the capabilities of the firing batteries. See figure 8-4 on page 114.

Firing Batteries

     8-7. Currently the batteries within the LRFB consist of a long-range hypersonic weapon battery, a mid-range
     capability battery, and a precision-range strike missile equipped HIMARS battery. The purpose of the LRHW
     battery is to suppress, neutralize, and destroy the enemy with ground-based strategic fires in support of the
     joint force in multidomain operations. The purpose of the mid-range capability battery is to suppress,
     neutralize, and destroy the enemy with ground-based mid-range fires in support of the joint force in
     multidomain operations. The purpose of the HIMARS battery is to suppress, neutralize, and destroy the
     enemy with FA rocket and missile fires.

LONG-RANGE HYPERSONIC WEAPON BATTERY

     8-8. The LRHW battery consists of a battery HQ, a battery operations center, two firing platoon HQ, four
     firing sections, a support platoon HQ, a supply section, and an ammunition management section. The battery
     will be positioned to allow for the most effective response with regards to its supported forces and correlation
     with the terrain, combat mission and responsiveness to higher headquarters.

     8-9. The LRHW battery engages strategic targets at range to generate desired effects. The LRHW battery can
     operate from designated PAA which consist of developed road networks, level/accessible surfaces, and close
     or distant ammunition supply points and ammunition holding areas. See figure 8-5 on page 115.

MID-RANGE CAPABILITY BATTERY

     8-10. The mid-range capability battery consists of a battery HQ, two platoon fire direction centers, four firing
     sections, a support platoon headquarters, a supply section, and an ammunition section. The battery will be
     positioned to allow for the most effective response with regards to its supported forces, and in correlation
     with terrain, combat mission, and responsiveness to higher HQ.

     8-11. The mid-range capability battery provides fires on mobile surface-to-surface targets in the land and
     maritime domains. These fires can consist of pre-planned targets and targets of opportunity that include but
     are not limited to enemy acquisition assets, command and control facilities, long-range theater missile
     systems, surface-to-air missile sites, and logistics support areas (in accordance with Army depth and
     simultaneous attack doctrine). See figure 8-6.

HIGH MOBILITY ARTILLERY ROCKET SYSTEM BATTERY

     8-12. The precision-range strike missile equipped HIMARS battery consists of a battery HQ, a battery
     operations center, three firing platoon HQ, nine firing sections, a support platoon HQ, supply section, and an
     ammunition management section. The battery will be positioned to allow for the most effective response with
     regards to its supported forces, and in correlation with terrain, combat mission, and responsiveness to higher
     headquarters.

     8-13. The HIMARS battery provides rocket and missile fires on pre-planned targets and targets of
     opportunity that include, but are not limited to enemy acquisition assets, command and control facilities,
     long-range theater missile systems, surface to air missile sites, logistics support areas, and armored mobile
     formations (in accordance with Army depth and simultaneous attack doctrine). See figure 8-7 on page 117.

SECTION II – STRYKER (155MM TOWED) CANNON BATTALIONS

     8-14. The Stryker battalion both regular Army and ARNG is organized with three M777 (155mm towed)
     howitzer batteries (3X6) in support of the Stryker brigade combat team (SBCT). The ARNG also has echelons
     above brigade FA battalions (3x4) 155mm towed batteries. The SBCT is an expeditionary combined arms
     force organized around mounted Infantry. SBCT units operate effectively in most terrain and weather
     conditions. The role of the SBCT is to close with the enemy trough fire and movement to destroy or capture
     enemy forces, or to repel enemy attacks by fire, close combat, and counterattack. The goal is to control land
     areas, including populations and resources. See FM 3-96 for more information on SBCT.

     8-15. The battalion has a HHB to provide C2 and administrative support. A FSC from a BSB provides
     logistical support. Figure 8-8 on page 118 shows the breakdown of a Stryker 155mm towed cannon battalion.
     The HHB for the 155mm towed cannon battalion is shown in figure 8-9 on page 118.

     8-16. Both regular Army and ARNG 155mm SP howitzer battalions provide support to armored brigade

     also have echelons above brigade FA battalions (3x6) batteries.

SECTION IV – HIGH MOBILITY ARTILLERY ROCKET SYSTEM AND MULTIPLE
    LAUNCH ROCKET SYSTEM BATTALIONS

     8-18. HIMARS and MLRS battalions in FABs provide long-range fires in support of the division, corps, joint
     task force, or other higher HQ decisive and shaping operations and for conducting counter fire. Rocket
     battalions are task organized against these specific mission requirements, often down to the battery or even
     platoon level.

     8-19. Rocket battalions can also provide supporting fires to subordinate elements (divisions or BCTs) as
     required in either a GS or GSR role but are rarely used to directly reinforce FA battalions in BCTs for
     operations other than wide area security. If used in a GSR or R role, restrictive ammunition CSRs are typically
     used to manage their employment.

HIGH MOBILITY ARTILLERY ROCKET SYSTEM BATTALION

     8-20. The mission of the HIMARS FA battalion is to provide timely, accurate rocket and missile fires in
     support of large-scale combat operations. The HIMARS is an air transportable, wheeled, indirect fire,
     rocket/missile system that can fire all rockets and missiles of the MLRS/HIMARS Family of Munitions.

commander and to reinforce the fires of other FA battalions. The battalions are assigned or attached to FABs
and DIVARTYs. The regular Army HIMARS battalions (see figure 8-12) are composed of a HHB, and three
firing batteries (3x9) each. The ARNG HIMARS battalions (see figure 8-13) are configured with an HHB,
and two firing batteries (2x8). See figure 8-14 on page 122 for an example of the HHB. A FSC from the BSB
provides logistical support.

MULTIPLE LAUNCH ROCKET SYSTEM BATTALION

     8-22. The M270 series MLRS is a highly mobile, lightly armored, tracked carrier vehicle with a launcher
     module mounted on the vehicle bed. It fires surface-to-surface rockets from the MLRS/HIMARS Family of
     Munitions. The regular Army and ARNG MLRS battalion consist of an HHB, and three firing batteries (3x9)
     each. For an example of an MLRS FA battalion see figure 8-15 on page 123 and an example of an HHB see
     figure 8-16 on page 123. A FSC from the BSB provides logistical support.

     8-23. The units are organized and equipped to provide FA rocket and missile fires for the supported
     commander and to reinforce the fires of other FA battalions. The battalions are assigned or attached to the
     FAB or DIVARTY. Additional units may be attached or assigned to a FAB or DIVARTY. The battalion is
     composed of a HHB, and three firing batteries with nine launchers each.

                                                Appendix A

             Field Artillery Battalion Mission Products

    This appendix discusses the FA battalion mission products. There are necessary inputs required
    to develop a FA battalion OPORD such as the higher HQ scheme of fires contained within their
    base OPORD and the Annex D (Fires). Depending on the tactical situation, supporting FA
    battalion usually conduct parallel planning with their higher HQ. Additionally, the FA battalion
    may utilize the FASP in lieu of a battalion OPORD. The following paragraphs provide an
    overview of the scheme of fires, Annex D (Fires), FA Battalion OPORD, and the FASP.

SCHEME OF FIRES

     A-1. The scheme of fires describes how the commander intends to use fires to support the concept of
     operations. It states the priorities for, allocation of, and restrictions on fires. The FSCOORD, FSO, targeting
     officer, and FSE develops the scheme of fires as part of the brigade's operations planning process. The scheme
     of fires is utilized by the FA battalion staff to develop the FA battalion OPORD. See ATP 3-09.42 for an
     example of a scheme of fires paragraph.

ANNEX D (FIRES)

     A-2. This annex provides fundamental considerations, format, and instructions for developing Annex D
     (Fires) to the base plan or order. This annex follows the five-paragraph attachment format. Commanders and
     staffs use Annex D (Fires) to describe how fires support the concept of operations described in the base plan
     or order. The FSCOORD, FSO, targeting officer, and FSE develops Annex D (Fires).

     A-3. This annex describes the fires concept of support objectives. A complex fires concept of support may
     require a schematic to show the fires objectives and task relationships. It includes a discussion of the overall
     fires concept of support with the specific details in element subparagraphs and attachments. It refers to the
     execution matrix to clarify timing relationships among various fires tasks. This annex also contains the
     information needed to synchronize timing relationships of each element related to fires. It includes fires
     related constraints, if appropriate. See ATP 3-09.42 for an example of a brigade annex D.

FIELD ARTILLERY BATTALION OPERATION ORDER

     A-4. An OPORD is a directive issued by a commander to subordinate commanders for the purpose of
     effecting the coordinated execution of an operation (JP 5-0). The purpose of the written FA battalion OPORD
     is to inform FA battalion subordinate and supporting units of tasks and requirements for a specific operation
     and to synchronize their involvement. The FA battalion OPORD is the FA battalion commander's tactical
     plan for employing fires. The FA battalion OPORD is an integral supporting document to the supported
     higher HQ OPORD and Annex D (Fires).

     A-5. The FA battalion OPORD explains the FA battalion command and support relationships and provides
     the FA battalion commander's guidance, intent, and concept of operations. It assigns responsibilities to
     subordinate and supporting elements, focusing on FA battalion actions necessary to execute FA tasks. The
     FA battalion OPORD must address the FA battalion commander's guidance, changes to fire order standards
     involving how and when to attack targets, and assignment of unit responsibilities for specific HPTs and other
     key targets or missions. The OPORD also addresses movement and positioning plans, C2 arrangements (for
     example, data links), target acquisition tasks, sustainment information, and protection measures.

     A-6. Appropriate annexes and appendices should be prepared for portions of the OPORD that are better
     explained in a different format (for example, overlay or matrix), that are too extensive to be in the OPORD,
     that are expected to change or lengthen, or that are submitted too late to be included in the OPORD. For more
     information on OPORD format see FM 5-0.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  125


---

Appendix A

FIELD ARTILLERY SUPPORT PLAN

A-7. This section is an example of the FASP. The section also highlights additional appendices that support
the FASP. A majority of the FASP is in Appendix 4 to Annex D to the OPORD. This appendix is not intended to
be all inclusive regarding the information required by an individual unit SOP. These outlines are recommended
for use by all FA battalions to standardize information within the FA community.

A-8. The FASP is the FA battalion commander’s tactical plan for employing the fires of all FA assets under
their span of responsibility. For a DS battalion this means the FASP must address all FA supporting the supported
force. It also means that the battalion must develop the FASP as an integral supporting document to the maneuver
plan, usually as an appendix to the fires annex of the maneuver OPORD. R, GSR, and GS battalions usually
prepare a stand-alone FASP focused on the battalion’s assets and mission execution.

A-9. The FASP explains the EFATs, and provides the FA battalion commander’s guidance, intent, and
concept of FA operations. The FASP assigns responsibilities to subordinate and supporting elements, focusing
on all tasks necessary to execute all EFATs in support of EFSTs. The FASP must address maneuver/FA
commander guidance, changes to fire order standards involving how and when to attack targets, and assignment
of unit responsibilities for specific HPTs and other key targets or missions. The FASP also addresses
movement/positioning plans, C2 arrangements (such as sensor-to-shooter links), target acquisition tasks, support
information, and force protection measures.

A-10. The battalion’s FASP generally does not duplicate information from the maneuver OPORD/FS plan or
a DS/higher FA HQ FASP, or from the battalion’s SOPs. However, it may be necessary to repeat critical
information in the battalion’s FASP if the higher-level FS plan/FASP or the battalion’s SOP are not disseminated
to all the battalion’s subordinate/supporting elements.

FIELD ARTILLERY SUPPORT PLAN FORMAT

A-11. The following pages provide an outline of the format for a FASP. The outline is annotated for specific
information requirements or suggestions. This format is available in automated C2 systems.

A-12. Reference: List any maps, charts, or other documents (SOP) required to understand the order. Reference
to a map will include the map series number (and country or geographic area, if required), sheet number (and
name if required), edition, scale (if required), and the force common datum (see note below) from a GPS (specify
type of GPS and datum used) or from the center of the lower margin on a map. Reference listed here should not
be reprinted in tabs unless tabs are separated from the basic document.

             Note. Universal transverse Mercator coordinates from the same point computed on a different
             datum may differ as much as 900 meters.

A-13. Time Zone Used Throughout Order: The time zone applicable to the operation. Times in other zones are
converted to this zone for this operation. Consistency must be maintained through all documents.

A-14. Paragraph 1. Situation: Paragraph 1 is used exclusively to provide information. It includes items of
information affecting FA operations that may or may not be included in the fires paragraph of the maneuver
OPORD/OPLAN or the FS annex. It gives an overview of the general situation so subordinate commanders can
understand the environment in which they will be operating. If all organic, attached, or supporting commanders
do not receive complete copies of the maneuver OPLAN/OPORD and the FS annex, then the FASP repeats those
items critical to the execution of their missions.

A-15. Sub-paragraph 1a. Enemy Forces: Subparagraph 1a provides enemy information vital to the FA unit.
This includes enemy indirect fire capabilities that may influence FS activities, the ground threat, the air threat,
counterfire threat, and any other enemy information of relevance to FA units. Reference may be made to an
intelligence annex, an overlay, a periodic intelligence report, or to an intelligence summary. Consider using the
intelligence annex as a tab.

A-16. Sub-paragraph 1b. Friendly Forces: Subparagraph 1b contains the mission and commander’s intent of
higher HQ (two levels up). The missions of adjacent, supporting, and R units also may be outlined here.
Information should be limited to that which subordinate commanders need to know to accomplish their missions.

126  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                                            Field Artillery Battalion Mission Products

The supported maneuver commander’s intent for FS as well as the force FA commander’s intent during GS and
GSR missions are included.

A-17. Sub-paragraph 1c. Attachments and Detachments: Sub-paragraph 1c should list units attached to and
detached from the FA unit (if not included clearly in the task organization), the terms of attachment, and effective
DTGs, if appropriate.

A-18. Sub-paragraph 1d. Assumptions: If the FASP supports an OPLAN, assumptions may be required. If the
FASP is part of an OPORD, assumptions are not included.

A-19. Paragraph 2. Mission: Paragraph 2 is a clear, concise statement of the task the FA unit is to accomplish.
As a minimum, it should answer the questions, who, what, when, where, and why. It includes essential tasks,
EFATs/EFSTs, determined by the commander as a result of their mission analysis.

A-20. Paragraph 3. Execution: Paragraph 3 contains the how-to information needed for mission
accomplishment. The FA battalion commander’s intent is expressed here. The intent should briefly address the
purpose of the FA fires, the methods used by the force to reach the end state to include EFSTs/EFATS that must
be accomplished, guidance on movements, methods of survivability, and the end state that addresses criteria for
success.

A-21. Sub-paragraph 3a. Concept of Operations: Subparagraph 3a is a detailed statement of the FA
commander’s visualization of the conduct of FA support for the operation by phase to the desired end state. The
concept clarifies the purpose of the operation (by phase), then how FA will support it. It is a detailed explanation
of the commander’s intent. It is stated in enough detail to ensure appropriate action by subordinate units in the
absence of more specific instructions. This paragraph may include a summary of the maneuver concept by phase
for units that do not receive the maneuver OPORD.

A-22. Sub-paragraph 3b. Organization for Combat: Subparagraph 3b is a clear statement of the organization
and support relationship or tactical missions of the subordinate units of the FA HQ. Organization for combat
normally will be done by various phases corresponding to the operation (if changes occur). Anticipated on-order
changes to organization, support relationships, or tactical missions are included in this subparagraph.

             Note. Subsequent subparagraphs in paragraph 3 build on the concept of the operation and should
             provide the artillery organization for combat, priority of fires, priority of special munitions,
             positioning and movement instructions, and EFATs to be accomplished by subordinate units. This
             should address each step of the operation.

A-23. Sub-paragraph 3c. Coordinating Instructions: The last subparagraph in paragraph 3 is coordinating
instructions. It includes instructions and details of coordination applicable to two or more subordinate FA units.
Instructions included in the subparagraph also may be addressed in tabs to the FASP. If a separate tab is developed,
include in the coordinating instructions subparagraph only items of general interest, with details placed in the tab.
If a tab is prepared, reference it in the body of the FASP. This subparagraph should include instructions concerning
the following:

          • Target acquisition to include counterfire, counterfire priorities, target acquisition asset positioning,
                and instructions to or about specific observers.

          • Survey includes priorities for survey, accuracies required, timing, position requirements.
          • Automated/manual fire control/fire direction instructions, especially coordination requirements with

                units with similar, but not identical, automated systems.
          • CBRN defense (includes MOPP, operation exposure guidance, and decontamination

                instructions/locations).
          • Meteorology (includes source, type, and times of meteorology messages).
          • Liaison requirements.
          • Ammunition restrictions (includes expenditure restrictions, approval requirements, and risk

                limitations).
                 Anti fratricide measures (such as vehicle markings) that are not SOP.
                 Rehearsals.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  127


---

Appendix A

A-24. Paragraph 4. Sustainment: Paragraph 4 includes specific service support instructions and arrangements
supporting the operation. The commander’s guidance regarding sustainment will be here. Supply, maintenance,
medical, and personnel information are included in this paragraph. As a minimum, the CSR and the sustainment
locations (combat trains, field trains, casualty collection points, LRP, ammunition transfer and holding point
and/or ammunition supply point) should be given. Address only those sustainment aspects that apply to the
operation. Address the pertinent aspects of the six tactical logistic functions of manning, arming, fueling, fixing,
moving and sustaining soldiers and their systems. These functions should be addressed before, during, and after
the operation and includes:

          • Sub-paragraph 4a. Manning: Current personnel strength (percentage), project replacements.
          • Sub-paragraph 4b. Arming: Ammunition resupply, CSR, ammunition transfer and holding point,

                ammunition supply point.
          • Sub-paragraph 4c. Fueling: Fuel points, priority of refuel.
          • Sub-paragraph 4d. Fixing: Unit maintenance control point location, allocation of recovery assets,

                maintenance and recovery priorities, cannibalization authority.
          • Sub-paragraph 4e. Moving: Locations of main supply route, start point, release point and

                checkpoints along the route, priorities of movement on main supply routes.
          • Sub-paragraph 4f. Sustaining: Medical resupply, barrier materials, restricted issue or controlled

                exchange items, mortuary affairs, Chaplain information, water points, locations of BAS, casualty
                collection point, CBRN casualty collection points, civil-military cooperation.

A-25.  Paragraph 5. Command and Signal includes:
       • Sub-paragraph 5a. Command: The first paragraph should list the locations of the unit CP and the

            locations of higher, supporting, and supported unit CPs. The commander’s planned location during
            the operation, and alternate CP can be in this paragraph. Plans should also address any alternate
            battalion FDC arrangements not covered in, or that are changed from, unit SOP. Other automated
            C2 systems used or interface requirements, and any aspects for their use also are included here.
            Address succession of command if other than SOP.
       • Sub-paragraph 5b. Signal: This sub-paragraph contains the index of the effective signal operating
            instructions to include edition in effect and courier schedule. Special instructions on the use of
            communications equipment, retransmission elements (location and priority) and signals not covered
            in SOP are included here.

A-26. Approval: The original/file copy bears the signature of the FA battalion commander. The S-3
authenticates all other copies, which are disseminated to subordinate and R/reinforced FA units, any attached or
supporting elements, and force FA HQ.

FIELD ARTILLERY SUPPORT PLAN TABS

A-27. Tabs should be prepared for portions of the FASP that are explained better in a different format (for
example, overlay or matrix), that are too extensive to be in the FASP, that are expected to change or lengthen, or
that are submitted too late to be included. Often subordinate units will not receive the basic maneuver
OPLAN/OPORD or FS annex. Reprints of portions of these documents may be required and included as tabs.

A-28. Tabs are ordered as they are referenced in the basic OPLAN or OPORD. The SOP may specify that some
tabs will always be produced. Common tabs used at battalion level include:

          • FASM.
          • FA positioning and movement overlay.
          • Survey.
          • Target acquisition.
          • Automated digital fires system.

FIELD ARTILLERY SUPPORT MATRIX TAB TO THE FASP

A-29. The FASM is a concise planning and execution tool that graphically depicts the FA unit’s essential tasks.
Like paragraph 3 of the written FASP, the matrix should answer the questions who, what, when, and where. It
identifies key tasks for each element and ties accomplishment of those tasks to the requirements of the maneuver
plan and the FS plan.

128                                ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                                                            Field Artillery Battalion Mission Products

FIELD ARTILLERY POSITIONING AND MOVEMENT OVERLAY

A-30. The FA positioning and movement overlay depicts the PAA (typically by phase of the operation), firing
unit locations, ranges, phase lines, and unit boundaries.

SURVEY TAB TO THE FASP

A-31. The survey plan (or order) normally is incorporated into the FASP as the survey tab. The survey plan
contains detailed instructions not covered by local TSOP for each survey team. It gives general information
needed for the efficient accomplishment of the survey mission. The survey plan is normally written, but may be
issued orally, if necessary. It generally follows the same format as the OPLAN/OPORD. Often because of the
tactical situation and wide dispersal of units, part of the survey plan may be issued by radio or other
communication means. The format for a survey plan is in ATP 3-09.02.

TARGET ACQUISITION TAB TO THE FIELD ARTILLERY SUPPORT PLAN

A-32. The target acquisition tab is a managerial tool used by the CP controlling FA target acquisition assets.
This tab is used to ensure that all target acquisition assets are employed to support the overall maneuver operation.
The purpose of the target acquisition tab is as follows:

          • Assigns missions to FA target acquisition assets.
          • Coordinates the FA target acquisition efforts.
          • Establishes a specific flow of target processing data.
          • Assigns responsibilities not covered in unit SOPs.

A-33. The FA battalion targeting officer supported by the S-2 and S-3 produce the target acquisition tab. For
specific information on the radar deployment order see ATP 3-09.12.

AUTOMATION TAB TO THE FIELD ARTILLERY SUPPORT PLAN

A-34. The written portion of the automation tab attached to the FASP should contain changes/additions to
subscriber information, and commander’s criteria, geometry, units, and ammunition information that pertain to
the operation (not contained in SOP).

A-35. Since automation systems provide fire planners throughout the artillery system with instantaneous access
to target files and FA schedules, the S-3 no longer must attach a fire plan tab (target list and schedules of fires) to
the written portion of the plan. Instead, he can initiate, coordinate, and monitor the development of the FA
schedules in accordance with the standard fire planning capabilities and procedures that apply to these systems.
In the written portion of the plan, he can reference FA schedules, target lists, and FSCM by plan name and, if
necessary, targets by target number. FA elements can extract detailed information as needed.

A-36. For an example of a FASP See figure A-1 on pages 130-133. For an example of a field artillery execution
matrix see figure A-2 on pages 134 -135. For an example of a field artillery positioning and movement overlay
see figure A-3 on page 135.
