                                                Appendix B

                            Liaison Operations

    This appendix provides an example of a liaison checklist that can be used when preparing for
    operations. This appendix includes a list of what the LNO should do before, during, and when
    departing the unit that they are providing liaison assistance.

PREPARATION FOR OPERATIONS

     B-1. The LNO directs the liaison team and represents the battalion commander at the supported unit. The
     LNO advises the supported commander on battalion capabilities, limitations, and disposition; recommends
     employment options and helps coordinate fires of the battalion with other FS assets. They keep their battalion
     commander informed on the current situation of the supported unit and on future requirements.

     B-2. LNOs are also responsible for the preparation, accountability and verification of equipment, and the
     training, welfare, and safety of the liaison team during operations. The following checklists can be utilized
     by battalion LNOs to conduct liaison duties. For more information on liaison operations see FM 3-0.

EQUIPMENT

     B-3. This checklist provides an example of the equipment needed for deployment or training exercises to
     include:

          • Personal field gear and equipment.
          • Night vision goggles.
          • GPS receiver.
          • Camouflage screening system.
          • Hex tent/stove/cots/water cans.
          • Current fire control system.
          • Communications Systems:

                 Radios/COMSEC devices.
                 Range enhancing antenna (2 each).
          • Maps and overlay material.
          • Field table with chairs.
          • Office supplies and materials to include:
                 Pens/pencils/markers.
                 Notepads and tablet.
                 Rubbing alcohol/paper towels.
                 DA Form 1594 (Daily Staff Journal or Duty Officer’s Log).
                 Fire mission logs.

INFORMATION

     B-4. This Checklist provides an example of the needed resources for secure communications, intelligence,
     and information to include:

          • SOP/SOI/OPLAN/OPORD of sending unit.
          • SOP/SOI/OPLAN/OPORD of receiving unit.
          • Location and route to receiving CP.
          • Intelligence updates.
          • Current plans, orders, maps, overlays, and targeting information to include concept of operation,

                concept of fires, and commander's intent.
          • Unit locations/readiness and strength.
          • Land management coordinating agency (force HQ FSE/fires element).
          • Logistical considerations and supporting agencies.
          • Current status of receiving unit's mission.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  137


---

Appendix B

DEPARTURE CHECKLIST

     B-5.    This checklist is utilized by the LNO before departing the parent unit to include:
          •     Checklist for commanders’ intent.
          •     Do you understand what the commander wants the receiving commander to know?
          •     Did you arrange for a briefing from operations, intelligence, and other staff elements concerning
                current and future operations?
          •     Did you verify the receipt of, and do you understand, the tasks your staff has given you?
          •     Did you obtain the correct maps, traces, and overlays (including maneuver, engineer, and FS
                overlays)?
          •     Did you arrange for transport, communications and cryptographic equipment, codes, and signal
                instructions, and for their protection and security?
          •     Did you arrange for the departure of the liaison party?
          •     Did you complete the route reconnaissance and time management plans so you will arrive at the
                designated location on time?
          •     Do you and your party know how you are to destroy the information you are carrying in an
                emergency, in transit, and at the receiving unit?
          •     Do you have an SOI? (Do you know the challenge and password?)
          •     Did you inform your HQ of when you will leave, what route you will take, when you are to arrive,
                POC for linkup with receiving unit, and when known, the estimated time and route of your return?
          •     Did you pick up all correspondence designated for the receiving HQ?
          •     Did you conduct a radio check? (Do you have appropriate COMSEC equipment?)
          •     Do you know the impending moves of your HQ and of the receiving HQ?
          •     Did you bring automation or computers to support your operation?
          •     Did you pack adequate supplies of Class I and III for use in transit?

LIAISON DUTIES CHECKLIST

     B-6.    This checklist in utilized by the LNO during liaison operations with the supported unit to include:
          •     Checklist notification of established operations.
          •     Establish and maintain communication(s) with sending unit.
          •     Notify your own HQ of your arrival.
          •     Deliver all correspondence designated for the receiving HQ.
          •     Visit staff elements, brief them about your unit, and collect information (such as maps, traces, and
                overlays) from them.
          •     Annotate on all overlays the security classification, title, map scale, grid intersection points, date
                time group information, date time group received, and from whom received.
          •     Participate in receiving unit's orders process, briefings, and rehearsals. Assist in development of:
                 FS execution matrix.
          •      FS plan.
          •     Advise on parent unit capabilities, requirements, limitations, and employment.
          •     Visit and coordinate routinely with all receiving unit staff elements.
                Send parent unit routine updates regarding mission, unit locations, future operations, and
          •     commander's intent.
          •     Organize work/rest cycle for 24-hour operations.
          •     Ensure that receiving unit S-3 is always aware of your location.
          •     Facilitate information exchange.
                Gather correspondence for your HQ before departure and inform the receiving HQ of when you will
                depart, what route you will take, and when you expect to arrive at the parent unit.

138                  ATP 3-09.23  25 February 2026


---

                                                Appendix C

                              Distributed Units

    This appendix provides a brief overview and discussion of techniques and associated
    considerations unique to batteries operating as distributed units. Long-range fires employment
    in support of theater strategic, and operational objectives will create a need for these units to
    operate in a distributed capacity. Distributed units are characterized as small units operating
    independently over extended distances, with or without additional support. These units are
    distributed across a large area to create advantages over enemy forces.

OVERVIEW

     C-1. Extensive dispersion reduces the unit's vulnerability to threat observation and fires but may increase
     its vulnerability to attack by enemy forces. Distributed units require effective leaders, decentralized positions,
     decentralized decision-making, and well-trained units.

     C-2. There are tactical advantages, disadvantages, and risks associated with distributed units. Small units
     operating independently, require improved external sustainment support from higher HQ. Distributed units
     require increased local security, improved situational awareness, reconnaissance and surveillance support,
     and organic sensors that can survive under fire. Increasing the distance between units beyond mutually
     supporting ranges may require support to supplement organic fires. Identify considerations associated with
     distributed units as early as possible in the MDMP.

     C-3. Small, distributed units present unique challenges and tactical considerations for the commander and
     key leaders. Considerations for distributed units include:

          • Method of control: centralized or decentralized.
          • Method of employment: section, platoons, pairs, or groups.
          • Personnel strength and level of training.
          • Communications equipment availability and level of training.
          • Sustainment supply, maintenance, and medical operations.

PERSONNEL

     C-4. Distributed units require small unit leaders that focus training on small unit doctrine, tactics,
     techniques, and procedures. These small unit leaders motivate, inspire, and influence their unit members to
     work toward a common purpose, compelling them to go beyond their individual interests and work for the
     common good. Distributed units require a greater reliance on teamwork, cohesion, and trust.

     C-5. Units may perform maneuver tasks in addition to firing tasks, as required. The FA unit is not equipped
     with the equipment, resources, or personnel to conduct standard infantry missions. A traditional infantry
     company is considerably larger that a battery. All personnel must fully understand the mission; cultural
     impacts, if any; and ROE. Unit SOP must be developed, rehearsed, and coordinated to effectively deal with
     these complex situations.

     C-6. The considerations for personnel will vary based on unit composition and method of employment. Unit
     modified tables of organization and equipment, task-organization, commander's guidance, personnel
     strength, level of training, individual capabilities, and other factors may require the commander to modify
     personnel assignments for task organization. The commander bases this decision on mission variables or unit
     SOP. The commander issues planning guidance on personnel, to include minimum safe manning levels for
     firing and priorities for defense of the unit. Considerations for unit personnel include:

          • Personnel strength levels.
                 Unit elements (distributed).
                 Unit positions (forward operating base, combat outpost, separate continental deployment).

          • Level of training on defense tactics and procedures.
          • Maneuver missions for one or more platoons.

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23  139


---

Appendix C

     • Monitor personnel strength levels for positions where the unit may not be able to fully maintain an
          effective defensive perimeter and provide for continuous support from local units.

HEALTH SERVICE SUPPORT

     C-7.    Considerations for health service support include:
          •
          •     Medical personnel attached to the unit and organization of combat medics to the platoons.
          •
                Determining the number of trained CLS personnel and quantity of CLS bags required.
          •
          •     Establish a selection criterion to identify the best-qualified battery members to attend training.
          •      Identification of sustainment and requalification training.
                 Identify and nominate unit members to attend emergency medical technician training to

                     enhance first responder care within the units.

                Pre-combat checks and inspections for availability and serviceability of the contents of CLS bags.

                Requisition Class VIII medical supplies for CLS bags and combat medic while attached.

                Identification of CASEVAC procedures including:
                 Number of litters (standard collapsible or nonstandard collapsible) for CASEVAC.
                 Identification of a platoon CASEVAC vehicle with litter securing equipment (example ratchet

                     straps or engineer tape).

     C-8. Request additional trained medical personnel to augment unit level medics to meet mission
     requirements. The goal should be to position a CLS and bag in every section and on every vehicle.

COMMUNICATIONS

     C-9. Distributed units require more robust communications equipment and assets to operate effectively over
     great distances. Communications between the launcher and the battery operation center is a major concern
     with increased distances. Develop unit SOP to establish primary and alternate means of communications and
     provide for redundancy.

     C-10. The unit’s communication requirements will vary based on unit composition and method of
     employment. Unit tables of organization and equipment, task-organization, commander's guidance,
     personnel strength, level of training, individual capabilities, and other factors may require the unit
     commander to modify the communications plan. The unit’s commander bases this decision on mission
     variables or unit SOP. The unit’s commander issues planning guidance on priorities for voice and data
     networks, to include redundancy.

     C-11.   Planning considerations for communications  include:
          •
          •     Communications equipment availability.
          •      Tactical radios.
                 Wire communications.
          •
          •     Information systems availability.
          •
          •     Network availability.
                 Voice networks.
                 Data networks.
                 Connectivity.
                 Bandwidth.
                 Redundancy.
                 Rehearsals.

                Training.
                 Unit training.
                 New equipment training.

                Certification.
                 Operators.
                 Unit leaders.

                Interference.

                Security measures.

140                     ATP 3-09.23                                25 February 2026


---

                                                                                                                         Distributed Units

C-12. Inventory tactical radios and wire communications, if available for accountability and serviceability.
Inventory non-organic equipment assigned to the unit, for example, theater provided equipment. Inventory
information systems, if available for accountability and serviceability. Inventory peripherals, power supplies,
and stand-alone kits, if equipped. Verify systems capability to maintain situational awareness, perform threat
analysis, and display imagery analysis.

C-13. Establish voice and data networks based on availability and operational status of equipment. Verify
network connectivity and bandwidth. Request additional bandwidth to support mission requirements, as
required. Establish backup plans for each network for redundancy. Conduct rehearsals of communications,
to include degraded operation.

C-14. Determine level of training and certification of unit personnel on assigned equipment. Identify training
requirements for communications equipment and information system operators. Request new equipment
training or mobile training teams, if available. Develop a certification program for communications
equipment or information system operators. Include unit leaders in training and certification.

C-15. Determine locations or terrain that may interfere with communications. Terrain masks may interfere
or disrupt lines of communication. Verify communications for future locations, if possible. Relocate to
improve communication with all elements and higher HQ.

C-16. Develop COMSEC measures to prevent disruption in connectivity. Use secure communications
equipment whenever possible. Practice communications discipline by limiting transmission length and
frequency of use.

C-17. Mission success depends as much on preparation as it does on planning. To make a tentative plan, the
unit commander must gather information by focusing on unit level mission variables. Mission checklists
could be valuable in providing sample topics and questions that may assist the commander in this effort.
Mission checklists include:

     • Mission planning.
     • Pre-combat checklists for:

           Ground threat (mounted and dismounted).
           Air threat.
           Counterfire.
           CBRN threats and hazards.
           MEDEVAC.
           Artillery raid procedures.
           Mines.
           Massing.
           Critical events timeline.
           Unit defense.
           Degraded operations.

  SECTION II – TERMS

airborne operation
          An operation involving the air movement into an objective area of combat forces and their logistic
          support for execution of a tactical, operational, or strategic mission. (JP 3-18)

ammunition transfer holding point
          A designated site operated by a brigade support battalion distribution company where ammunition is
          received, temporarily stored, or transferred to supported units within a functional or multifunctional
          brigade. Also called ATHP. (ATP 4-35)

civil reconnaissance
          A targeted, planned, and coordinated observation and evaluation of specific civil aspects of the
          environment such as areas, structures, capabilities, organizations, people, or events. (JP 3-57)

*controlled supply rate
          The rate of ammunition consumption that can be supported, considering availability, facilities, and
          transportation. It is expressed in rounds per unit, individual, or vehicle per day.

direct support
          A support relationship requiring a force to support another specific force and authorizing it to answer
          directly to the supported force's request for assistance. (FM 3-0)

forward observer
          An individual operating with front line troops trained to adjust ground or naval gunfire and pass back
          battlefield information. Also called FO. (JP 3-09)

joint fires observer
          A certified and qualified Service member who requests, controls, and adjusts surface-to-surface fires;
          provides targeting information in support of close air support; and performs terminal guidance
          operations. Also called JFO. (JP 3-09.3)

reinforcing
          A support relationship requiring a force to support another supporting unit. (FM 3-0)

*required supply rate
          The amount of ammunition expressed in terms of rounds per weapon per day for ammunition items
          fired by weapons, in terms of other units of measure per day for bulk allotment, and other items
          estimated to be required to sustain operations of any designated force without restriction for a specified
          period.

support area
          The portion of the commander’s area of operations that is designated to facilitate the positioning,
          employment, and protection of base sustainment assets required to sustain, enable, and control
          operations. (ADP 3-0)

survivability
          A quality or capability of military forces which permits them to avoid or withstand hostile actions or
          environmental conditions while retaining the ability to fulfill their primary mission. (ATP 3-37.34)

25 February 2026  ATP 3-09.23                    145

Glossary

survivability operations
          Those protection activities that alter the physical environment by providing or improving cover,
          camouflage, and concealment. (ATP 3-37.34)

sustainment maintenance
          Off-system component repair and/or end item repair and return to the supply system or by exception to
          the owning unit, performed by national level maintenance providers. (ATP 4-33)
